### HALoS: Hierarchical Asynchronous Local SGD over Slow Networks for Geo-Distributed Large Language Model Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04531v1)
- **Authors**: Geon-Woo Kim, Junbo Li, Shashidhar Gandham, Omar Baldonado, Adithya Gangidi, Pavan Balaji, Zhangyang Wang, Aditya Akella
- **Abstract**: Training large language models (LLMs) increasingly relies on geographically distributed accelerators, causing prohibitive communication costs across regions and uneven utilization of heterogeneous hardware. We propose HALoS, a hierarchical asynchronous optimization framework that tackles these issues by introducing local parameter servers (LPSs) within each region and a global parameter server (GPS) that merges updates across regions. This hierarchical design minimizes expensive inter-region communication, reduces straggler effects, and leverages fast intra-region links. We provide a rigorous convergence analysis for HALoS under non-convex objectives, including theoretical guarantees on the role of hierarchical momentum in asynchronous training. Empirically, HALoS attains up to 7.5x faster convergence than synchronous baselines in geo-distributed LLM training and improves upon existing asynchronous methods by up to 2.1x. Crucially, HALoS preserves the model quality of fully synchronous SGD-matching or exceeding accuracy on standard language modeling and downstream benchmarks-while substantially lowering total training time. These results demonstrate that hierarchical, server-side update accumulation and global model merging are powerful tools for scalable, efficient training of new-era LLMs in heterogeneous, geo-distributed environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HALOS, a hierarchical asynchronous local SGD framework designed for efficient geo-distributed large language model (LLM) training.  HALOS tackles the challenges of slow inter-region communication and hardware heterogeneity by deploying local parameter servers (LPSs) within each region and a global parameter server (GPS) for merging updates.  This structure minimizes expensive inter-region communication, reduces the impact of stragglers, and leverages fast intra-region links.  The paper provides a convergence analysis for HALOS under non-convex objectives, including theoretical guarantees on the role of hierarchical momentum.  Empirical results demonstrate faster convergence than synchronous baselines and existing asynchronous methods while preserving model quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core idea of hierarchical asynchronous training isn't entirely new, as federated learning has explored similar structures. However, applying it specifically to *geo-distributed* LLM training with the emphasis on server-side update accumulation and global model merging introduces a distinct contribution.  The convergence analysis, particularly the in-depth consideration of momentum and delays in a hierarchical asynchronous setting, represents a significant theoretical advance.  The focus on practical challenges like slow inter-region communication, combined with the performance gains, strengthens the practical relevance of the approach.
* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a critical and increasingly important problem: scaling LLM training beyond single-datacenter setups.  The practical improvements in training time (up to 7.5x faster convergence) are substantial and directly relevant to real-world LLM development. The retention of accuracy compared to synchronous SGD is also very important for adoption.  The detailed analysis of the impact of hyperparameters (momentum, update frequency, merging weight) provides valuable insights for practitioners. The robust testing across several models and datasets is another positive.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents convincing empirical evidence of HALOS's effectiveness, including comparisons with relevant baselines (DiLoCo and Async-Local-SGD). The experiments focus on practical metrics like wall-clock time and token consumption.
*   **Solid Theoretical Foundation:** The convergence analysis adds rigor and offers insights into the behavior of HALOS, particularly regarding the role of momentum and delays.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The paper directly addresses the challenges of geo-distributed LLM training, a growing area of interest and importance.
*   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide a comprehensive understanding of the impact of different components and hyperparameters within HALOS.
*  **Open-Source Implementation:** This allows for easier adoption and validation of the results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Comparison Set:** While the baselines chosen are reasonable, a more comprehensive comparison against other distributed training techniques (e.g., some advanced model parallelism strategies tailored for geo-distributed environments) could strengthen the claims.
* **Heterogeneous Cluster configuration details:** While it mentions using different speeds for workers, the configuration details are a little sparse. A breakdown showing how this speed translates to hardware and model would be a strong positive.

**Potential Influence:**

HALOS has the potential to influence the way LLMs are trained, particularly in scenarios where access to large, co-located GPU clusters is limited or prohibitively expensive. The framework's ability to leverage geographically distributed resources could democratize LLM development, making it more accessible to a wider range of organizations. The theoretical analysis could also spur further research into asynchronous optimization methods for LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a technically sound and practically relevant contribution to the field of distributed LLM training. The combination of a novel hierarchical asynchronous architecture, rigorous convergence analysis, and compelling empirical results warrants a high score. While the novelty isn't revolutionary (drawing inspiration from federated learning), the specific application to geo-distributed LLMs and the detailed analysis of momentum and delays make it a significant advance. The limitations mentioned above (limited comparison set, slightly sparse experiment descriptions) prevent it from achieving a perfect score.

### hdl2v: A Code Translation Dataset for Enhanced LLM Verilog Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04544v1)
- **Authors**: Charles Hong, Brendan Roberts, Huijae An, Alex Um, Advay Ratan, Yakun Sophia Shao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are playing an increasingly large role in domains such as code generation, including hardware code generation, where Verilog is the key language. However, the amount of publicly available Verilog code pales in comparison to the amount of code available for software languages like Python. In this work, we present hdl2v ("HDL-to-Verilog"), a dataset which seeks to increase the amount of available human-written Verilog data by translating or compiling three other hardware description languages - VHDL, Chisel, and PyMTL3 - to Verilog. Furthermore, we demonstrate the value of hdl2v in enhancing LLM Verilog generation by improving performance of a 32 billion-parameter open-weight model by up to 23% (pass@10) in VerilogEvalV2, without utilizing any data augmentation or knowledge distillation from larger models. We also show hdl2v's ability to boost the performance of a data augmentation-based fine-tuning approach by 63%. Finally, we characterize and analyze our dataset to better understand which characteristics of HDL-to-Verilog datasets can be expanded upon in future work for even better performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "hdl2v: A Code Translation Dataset for Enhanced LLM Verilog Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces hdl2v, a new dataset designed to improve the Verilog code generation capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The dataset consists of approximately 46,000 pairs of hardware description language (HDL) code (VHDL, Chisel, and PyMTL3) and their corresponding Verilog translations. The authors demonstrate that fine-tuning LLMs on hdl2v leads to significant performance gains on the VerilogEvalV2 benchmark, even without employing more advanced techniques like data augmentation or knowledge distillation. They also explore how hdl2v can enhance existing data augmentation methods. The paper also provides statistical analysis of the dataset and ablation studies to understand the characteristics of effective HDL-to-Verilog training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Dataset:** The primary contribution is the creation and release of a substantial HDL-to-Verilog translation dataset. Given the relative scarcity of publicly available Verilog code compared to software languages like Python, this dataset addresses a significant bottleneck in training LLMs for hardware design tasks.
    *   **Demonstrated Improvement:** The paper convincingly shows that fine-tuning LLMs with hdl2v demonstrably improves Verilog generation performance. The gains on VerilogEvalV2 are significant and are further emphasized by the enhancements the dataset brings to existing data augmentation techniques.
    *   **Ablation Studies and Analysis:** The paper doesn't just present the dataset; it attempts to understand *why* it works. The analysis of dataset statistics (perplexity, token diversity) and the ablation studies (C vs VHDL, comment removal, variable obfuscation) provide insights into the characteristics of effective training data for LLMs in this domain. This adds value beyond just providing a dataset; it provides guidance for future research.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, clearly explains the methodology, and presents the results in a straightforward manner. The figures and tables are helpful in understanding the key findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Investigation:**  While the improvements are significant, the study primarily focuses on one LLM (Qwen2.5-Coder) and one benchmark (VerilogEvalV2).  Exploring the performance gains on other LLMs (especially closed-source ones, or ones trained on different code corpora) and other hardware generation benchmarks would strengthen the generality of the results.  The paper doesn't extensively examine failure cases or types of Verilog code that still pose challenges to the fine-tuned models.
    *   **Dataset limitations:** Although the dataset significantly increases the availability of translated Verilog data, the reliance on automated translation or compilation introduces potential biases and artifacts.  There is no rigorous evaluation of the correctness or quality of the translated Verilog code in the dataset itself.  It would be beneficial to manually check a subset of the translation to ensure high quality.
    *   **Incremental Advance:** While the dataset is novel, the overall approach of fine-tuning LLMs on code datasets is not. The work builds upon existing methods in the code generation and LLM fine-tuning literature.  The data augmentation study is a useful demonstration, but it is fairly standard in its methodology.
    *   **GPT-4o Use Limitations:** The data augmentation section (using GPT-4o) involves a potentially expensive, and non-reproducible operation due to dependency on external services. Releasing the generated dataset with added description could avoid reliance on GPT-4o services.

*   **Significance:**

    The hdl2v dataset represents a valuable resource for the hardware design community. By increasing the availability of Verilog training data, it enables researchers and engineers to develop more effective LLM-based tools for hardware design automation. The insights from the dataset analysis contribute to a better understanding of how LLMs learn to generate Verilog code. The dataset also has practical benefits as well, and can greatly improve workflows for engineers.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper. While the dataset itself is a significant contribution and the experimental results are compelling, the paper falls short of being truly groundbreaking. It demonstrates the effectiveness of a relatively straightforward fine-tuning approach using a new dataset, but it does not introduce radically new techniques or theoretical insights. The scope of the experimentation, while solid, is somewhat limited, and the reliance on potentially biased, automated translation is a notable weakness. The significance is nonetheless tangible, and the improvements are apparent. As such, it lands at a reasonable position.

### Perceptual Decoupling for Scalable Multi-modal Reasoning via Reward-Optimized Captioning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04559v1)
- **Authors**: Yunhao Gou, Kai Chen, Zhili Liu, Lanqing Hong, Xin Jin, Zhenguo Li, James T. Kwok, Yu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in slow-thinking language models (e.g., OpenAI-o1 and DeepSeek-R1) have demonstrated remarkable abilities in complex reasoning tasks by emulating human-like reflective cognition. However, extending such capabilities to multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) remains challenging due to the high cost of retraining vision-language alignments when upgrading the underlying reasoner LLMs. A straightforward solution is to decouple perception from reasoning, i.e., converting visual inputs into language representations (e.g., captions) that are then passed to a powerful text-only reasoner. However, this decoupling introduces a critical challenge: the visual extractor must generate descriptions that are both faithful to the image and informative enough to support accurate downstream reasoning. To address this, we propose Reasoning-Aligned Perceptual Decoupling via Caption Reward Optimization (RACRO) - a reasoning-guided reinforcement learning strategy that aligns the extractor's captioning behavior with the reasoning objective. By closing the perception-reasoning loop via reward-based optimization, RACRO significantly enhances visual grounding and extracts reasoning-optimized representations. Experiments on multi-modal math and science benchmarks show that the proposed RACRO method achieves state-of-the-art average performance while enabling superior scalability and plug-and-play adaptation to more advanced reasoning LLMs without the necessity for costly multi-modal re-alignment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Reasoning-Aligned Perceptual Decoupling via Caption Reward Optimization (RACRO), a framework for improving multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) by decoupling visual perception from reasoning. The core idea is to convert visual inputs into language descriptions (captions) that are then processed by a powerful text-only language model.  To ensure high-quality, reasoning-relevant captions, RACRO uses a reinforcement learning strategy (Caption Reward Optimization - CRO) to fine-tune the captioning extractor. CRO uses the downstream reasoning performance as a reward signal, aligning the extractor's captioning behavior with the reasoning objective.  Experiments on multi-modal math and science benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art performance and improved scalability and plug-and-play adaptability to advanced reasoning LLMs. The key benefit is the ability to leverage more powerful text-only LLMs for reasoning without requiring costly multi-modal retraining.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of decoupling perception and reasoning isn't entirely new. Existing methods convert visual data into intermediate textual representations. However, the crucial novelty lies in the *reasoning-aligned* optimization of the captioning process.  Instead of relying on generic image captions, the CRO method ensures the generated captions are directly relevant and informative *for the specific reasoning task at hand*. This is a significant improvement over existing decoupling techniques. Furthermore, the framework's ability to seamlessly integrate with and scale to different, potentially more powerful, text-based LLMs without re-aligning vision-language connectors is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in the field of MLLMs: the high cost of multi-modal training when upgrading the underlying LLM.  RACRO offers a practical solution by enabling the use of more advanced (and evolving) LLMs for reasoning without the need for computationally expensive vision-language re-alignment.  The performance improvements demonstrated on challenging benchmarks (MathVerse, MathVision, LogicVista) suggest that RACRO can significantly enhance the reasoning capabilities of MLLMs. The plug-and-play nature of the approach has a potential broader impact, enabling faster adoption of LLM advancements in multi-modal systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on several challenging benchmarks, supporting the effectiveness of the RACRO framework. The Pareto front analysis comparing RACRO-enhanced model with different MLLMs is convincing.
    *   **Well-Defined Method:** The methodology is clearly explained, with a detailed description of the CRO algorithm and its components. The provided ablation studies offer insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Practicality and Scalability:** RACRO offers a practical solution to a real-world problem in MLLM development. The method's scalability is a major selling point, especially given the rapid advancements in LLMs.
    *   **Thorough Experiments and Ablations:** The extensive experiments, including ablation studies, pairwise caption quality comparisons, and analysis of training dynamics, support the claims made in the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalization Discussion:** While the paper shows strong results on math and science tasks, the generalization of RACRO to other types of multi-modal reasoning tasks (e.g., visual question answering on real-world images, embodied AI) is not thoroughly explored.
    *   **Dependence on MLLM Extractor:**  The method still relies on an MLLM as the initial visual extractor.  The performance is inherently limited by the initial alignment of this module, even though it mitigates further costly alignments. Further research into the quality of the MLLM's vision component and alternatives would strengthen the work.
    *   **Reward Computation:** The reward computation heavily relies on the performance of an LLM reasoner and parser. This can potentially introduce noise and biases if the LLM reasoner and parser have limitations or are not well-suited to the specific task.

*   **Justification:** While the idea of decoupling is not entirely novel, the way RACRO approaches it -- reasoning-aligned optimization -- is innovative. It offers a practical and scalable solution that leverages the strengths of LLMs in a computationally efficient way. The experimental results validate its effectiveness. However, limitations concerning generalization and the dependence on an MLLM extractor prevent a higher score.

### From Standalone LLMs to Integrated Intelligence: A Survey of Compound Al Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04565v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayi Chen, Junyi Ye, Guiling Wang
- **Abstract**: Compound Al Systems (CAIS) is an emerging paradigm that integrates large language models (LLMs) with external components, such as retrievers, agents, tools, and orchestrators, to overcome the limitations of standalone models in tasks requiring memory, reasoning, real-time grounding, and multimodal understanding. These systems enable more capable and context-aware behaviors by composing multiple specialized modules into cohesive workflows. Despite growing adoption in both academia and industry, the CAIS landscape remains fragmented, lacking a unified framework for analysis, taxonomy, and evaluation. In this survey, we define the concept of CAIS, propose a multi-dimensional taxonomy based on component roles and orchestration strategies, and analyze four foundational paradigms: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), LLM Agents, Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), and orchestration-centric architectures. We review representative systems, compare design trade-offs, and summarize evaluation methodologies across these paradigms. Finally, we identify key challenges-including scalability, interoperability, benchmarking, and coordination-and outline promising directions for future research. This survey aims to provide researchers and practitioners with a comprehensive foundation for understanding, developing, and advancing the next generation of system-level artificial intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the survey paper on Compound AI Systems:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a survey on Compound AI Systems (CAIS), which integrate Large Language Models (LLMs) with external components like retrievers, agents, and orchestrators. It defines CAIS, proposes a multi-dimensional taxonomy based on component roles and orchestration strategies, and analyzes foundational paradigms including Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), LLM Agents, Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), and orchestration-centric architectures. The paper reviews representative systems, compares design trade-offs, summarizes evaluation methodologies, identifies key challenges (scalability, interoperability, benchmarking, and coordination), and outlines future research directions. The aim is to provide a comprehensive foundation for understanding, developing, and advancing system-level artificial intelligence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in providing a unified framework and taxonomy for CAIS. While individual components like RAG, LLM Agents, and MLLMs have been surveyed before, the paper attempts to integrate them into a cohesive system-level perspective.  This holistic view is valuable, as it helps to understand the interactions and trade-offs between different components within the broader CAIS architecture. The notion of "Orchestration of Compound AI Systems" as a distinct dimension is also a valuable contribution, highlighting the importance of system design.
*   **Significance:**  CAIS is undeniably a significant trend in AI. Addressing the limitations of standalone LLMs through modular and composable systems is crucial for real-world deployments. The paper's analysis of architectural blueprints, coordination strategies, and recurring design patterns is valuable for both researchers and practitioners. Identifying challenges and outlining a research agenda further adds to its practical significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey covers a wide range of relevant literature.
    *   **Clear Taxonomy:** The proposed taxonomy provides a structured way to analyze and compare different CAIS architectures. The four orthogonal axes are well-defined.
    *   **Practical Insights:** Identifying recurring design patterns, trade-offs, and failure modes provides valuable practical insights.
    *   **Future Research Agenda:** The paper outlines open challenges and future directions, which is essential for guiding future research efforts.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Depth vs. Breadth:**  While comprehensive, the paper might benefit from a deeper dive into specific aspects of CAIS. The discussions on evaluation metrics for different component paradigms seem somewhat cursory and could be strengthened with more detailed examples and comparative analyses.
    *   **Evaluation of Framework:** The evaluation framework is only briefly mentioned. A more detailed exploration of how to assess CAIS, specifically regarding robustness, safety, and human-centered utility would add significant value. What are the metrics, tasks, and datasets for each? How do they combine?
    *   **Emerging trends:** The survey could expand to address some of the latest trends, such as incorporating self-supervised learning into CAIS and the evolution of component architecture, as these technologies are fast emerging and transforming in CAIS.
    *   **Limited critical assessment**: The paper could add more critical assessment. For each of the components what are the limitations and problems that still need to be addressed in research?
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a common language and framework for CAIS research. It can guide the development of more modular, composable, and reliable AI systems.
*   **Justification of Score:** While the paper provides a useful overview and framework, it could benefit from more in-depth analysis and a more robust evaluation framework. The existing literature is surveyed effectively, but the synthesis could be taken further to provide more actionable guidance to those designing and deploying CAIS. The potential influence of the survey is significant, however there is room for improving rigor in certain areas to better capture a comprehensive critical landscape of the existing architectures.

### OpenAg: Democratizing Agricultural Intelligence
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04571v1)
- **Authors**: Srikanth Thudumu, Jason Fisher
- **Abstract**: Agriculture is undergoing a major transformation driven by artificial intelligence (AI), machine learning, and knowledge representation technologies. However, current agricultural intelligence systems often lack contextual understanding, explainability, and adaptability, especially for smallholder farmers with limited resources. General-purpose large language models (LLMs), while powerful, typically lack the domain-specific knowledge and contextual reasoning needed for practical decision support in farming. They tend to produce recommendations that are too generic or unrealistic for real-world applications. To address these challenges, we present OpenAg, a comprehensive framework designed to advance agricultural artificial general intelligence (AGI). OpenAg combines domain-specific foundation models, neural knowledge graphs, multi-agent reasoning, causal explainability, and adaptive transfer learning to deliver context-aware, explainable, and actionable insights. The system includes: (i) a unified agricultural knowledge base that integrates scientific literature, sensor data, and farmer-generated knowledge; (ii) a neural agricultural knowledge graph for structured reasoning and inference; (iii) an adaptive multi-agent reasoning system where AI agents specialize and collaborate across agricultural domains; and (iv) a causal transparency mechanism that ensures AI recommendations are interpretable, scientifically grounded, and aligned with real-world constraints. OpenAg aims to bridge the gap between scientific knowledge and the tacit expertise of experienced farmers to support scalable and locally relevant agricultural decision-making.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OpenAg, a framework designed to democratize agricultural intelligence by addressing limitations in current AI applications for agriculture, particularly for smallholder farmers. OpenAg aims to integrate disparate agricultural data sources, provide explainable AI-driven recommendations, and adapt to diverse agricultural contexts. It achieves this through a combination of: (1) a unified agricultural knowledge base (UAKB), (2) a neural agricultural knowledge graph engine (NAKGG), (3) an adaptive multi-agent reasoning system (AMRS), (4) causal decision transparency (CADET), and (5) adaptive agricultural transfer learning (AATL). The framework emphasizes contextual understanding, explainability, and adaptability, aiming to bridge the gap between scientific knowledge and farmer expertise.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's strength lies in its comprehensive approach. While individual components like knowledge graphs, multi-agent systems, and transfer learning are not entirely novel in isolation, their integration into a unified framework specifically tailored for the challenges of agriculture represents a significant advance. The focus on democratizing agricultural intelligence by addressing the needs of smallholder farmers and incorporating tacit knowledge is also noteworthy. The synthesis of neural knowledge graphs with causal explainability mechanisms also demonstrates a good approach. However, the paper does not present novel methods on each of the individual components. Instead it synthesizes existing technologies and frameworks. The paper introduces an architecture and a vision, rather than specific algorithms and techniques.

*   **Significance:** If successfully implemented, OpenAg has the potential to significantly impact agricultural practices, particularly in resource-constrained environments. By providing context-aware, explainable, and adaptable decision support, OpenAg can empower farmers to make more informed decisions, optimize resource utilization, and improve sustainability. Addressing data heterogeneity and explainability limitations of current AI systems also makes it potentially valuable for practitioners. The paper acknowledges the importance of stakeholder buy-in and trust. However, the significance hinges on the practical implementation and validation of the framework. The effectiveness of the transfer learning and the scalability of the multi-agent system need to be demonstrated.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:**  The paper presents a well-defined and holistic architecture that addresses the key challenges in agricultural AI.
    *   **Emphasis on Explainability and Adaptability:** The framework explicitly prioritizes these crucial aspects, which are often overlooked in existing systems.
    *   **Focus on Democratization:** OpenAg aims to make advanced agricultural intelligence accessible to a wider range of stakeholders, including smallholder farmers.
    *   **Clear Articulation of Challenges:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the limitations of current AI applications in agriculture.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Lack of Implementation Details:** The paper primarily focuses on the architectural design of OpenAg and lacks detailed information on its implementation. More concrete examples and algorithms would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Empirical Validation:** The paper does not present empirical results to demonstrate the effectiveness of OpenAg in real-world agricultural settings.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** The multi-agent reasoning system could face scalability challenges when deployed in large and complex agricultural systems.
    *   **Overly Ambitious Goals:** Calling the framework a step toward "Artificial General Intelligence" (AGI) in agriculture might be an overstatement given the current state of the framework.

*   **Potential Influence:** If OpenAg is successfully implemented, it could influence the direction of research in agricultural AI by emphasizing the importance of contextual understanding, explainability, adaptability, and knowledge democratization. It could also lead to the development of new AI-powered decision support tools that are more accessible and useful for farmers.

**Justification:** The score reflects the novelty of the *integrated framework* specifically aimed at tackling key limitations within the agricultural context. The architecture synthesizes existing technologies, but applies them towards a very specific problem domain with emphasis on real-world practicalities and stakeholder needs. However, the lack of experimental results and clear implementation steps lowers the score.

### Demonstrations of Integrity Attacks in Multi-Agent Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04572v1)
- **Authors**: Can Zheng, Yuhan Cao, Xiaoning Dong, Tianxing He
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in natural language understanding, code generation, and complex planning. Simultaneously, Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) have garnered attention for their potential to enable cooperation among distributed agents. However, from a multi-party perspective, MAS could be vulnerable to malicious agents that exploit the system to serve self-interests without disrupting its core functionality. This work explores integrity attacks where malicious agents employ subtle prompt manipulation to bias MAS operations and gain various benefits. Four types of attacks are examined: \textit{Scapegoater}, who misleads the system monitor to underestimate other agents' contributions; \textit{Boaster}, who misleads the system monitor to overestimate their own performance; \textit{Self-Dealer}, who manipulates other agents to adopt certain tools; and \textit{Free-Rider}, who hands off its own task to others. We demonstrate that strategically crafted prompts can introduce systematic biases in MAS behavior and executable instructions, enabling malicious agents to effectively mislead evaluation systems and manipulate collaborative agents. Furthermore, our attacks can bypass advanced LLM-based monitors, such as GPT-4o-mini and o3-mini, highlighting the limitations of current detection mechanisms. Our findings underscore the critical need for MAS architectures with robust security protocols and content validation mechanisms, alongside monitoring systems capable of comprehensive risk scenario assessment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Demonstrations of Integrity Attacks in Multi-Agent Systems" systematically analyzes integrity attacks in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems (MAS).  It identifies four distinct attack archetypes: Scapegoater (misleading monitor to underestimate others), Boaster (overestimating self-performance), Self-Dealer (manipulating others to adopt certain tools), and Free-Rider (offloading tasks). The authors empirically demonstrate that strategically crafted prompts can introduce biases in MAS, enabling malicious agents to mislead evaluation systems and manipulate collaborative agents.  They find that even advanced LLM-based monitors (GPT-4o-mini and 03-mini) are susceptible to these attacks, highlighting limitations in current detection mechanisms. The paper evaluates these attacks across CAMEL, AutoGen, and MetaGPT in code generation, mathematical problem-solving, and knowledge-based QA. The authors further attempt to mitigate the attacks with explicit monitor instructions, but even these defense mechanisms fail. The paper concludes by emphasizing the need for robust security protocols and content validation mechanisms within MAS architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its focused examination of *integrity* attacks specifically within the context of LLM-based MAS. While adversarial attacks on LLMs and MAS are explored in literature, the paper focuses on subtle manipulations and biases without overtly disrupting functionality, which hasn't been deeply researched. The four attack archetypes offer a useful framework for categorizing these subtle manipulation strategies.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical security concern: the vulnerability of collaborative LLM systems to manipulation that undermines fairness and trustworthiness *without* breaking the system. This is particularly relevant as MAS become more widespread in open and multi-party environments where agents may have conflicting interests. The demonstration that even advanced LLM-based monitors are susceptible to such attacks has significant implications for the deployment of MAS in real-world scenarios. The fact that attacks can be realized with manually crafted prompts points to a possible lack of robustness of LLMs towards specific conversation flows.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Analysis:**  The paper presents a clear and well-structured analysis of the different attack types.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The attacks are empirically tested across multiple MAS frameworks and tasks, lending credibility to the findings.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The identified vulnerabilities are realistic concerns for MAS deployments, addressing scenarios where incentives align poorly.
    *   **Attempts at Defense:** The inclusion of defensive strategies (even if unsuccessful) adds to the completeness and rigor of the work. The failure of simple mitigation strategies emphasizes the difficulty and importance of the problem.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Automation of Attacks:** The attacks primarily rely on manually crafted prompts. The automated experiments towards the end are not as developed as the manual crafting. It would have strengthened the paper if the process of *finding* these attack prompts was more automated, perhaps through some adversarial search algorithm.
    *   **Depth of Defense:**  While the paper points out the problem of detecting these attacks, it doesn't propose deep defense mechanisms. A more theoretical or algorithmic solution to the problem would add significant value. It primarily tests the addition of extra instructions.
    *   **Generalizability of Prompts:** While different systems and task domains are considered, the prompts themselves may not generalize well across all possible MAS.
    *   **Limited exploration of scale:** The scale of the experiments, in terms of the number of agents involved, could be scaled up to better understand the behavior of the proposed attacks on more complicated systems.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This paper has the potential to influence the design and development of more secure and trustworthy LLM-based MAS. It raises awareness among researchers and practitioners about the subtle ways in which MAS can be manipulated, thereby motivating the development of more robust architectures and monitoring systems. It could spur research into novel validation and auditing techniques tailored for collaborative LLM systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a clear, relevant issue with sufficient empirical grounding. While it could benefit from greater attack automation and more substantial defensive strategies, the paper is a useful contribution to the community by highlighting the vulnerabilities in a rapidly developing field. It would have scored higher had it presented more elaborate defenses or more sophisticated methods of attack automation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reasoning or Overthinking: Evaluating Large Language Models on Financial Sentiment Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04574v1)
- **Authors**: Dimitris Vamvourellis, Dhagash Mehta
- **Abstract**: We investigate the effectiveness of large language models (LLMs), including reasoning-based and non-reasoning models, in performing zero-shot financial sentiment analysis. Using the Financial PhraseBank dataset annotated by domain experts, we evaluate how various LLMs and prompting strategies align with human-labeled sentiment in a financial context. We compare three proprietary LLMs (GPT-4o, GPT-4.1, o3-mini) under different prompting paradigms that simulate System 1 (fast and intuitive) or System 2 (slow and deliberate) thinking and benchmark them against two smaller models (FinBERT-Prosus, FinBERT-Tone) fine-tuned on financial sentiment analysis. Our findings suggest that reasoning, either through prompting or inherent model design, does not improve performance on this task. Surprisingly, the most accurate and human-aligned combination of model and method was GPT-4o without any Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting. We further explore how performance is impacted by linguistic complexity and annotation agreement levels, uncovering that reasoning may introduce overthinking, leading to suboptimal predictions. This suggests that for financial sentiment classification, fast, intuitive "System 1"-like thinking aligns more closely with human judgment compared to "System 2"-style slower, deliberative reasoning simulated by reasoning models or CoT prompting. Our results challenge the default assumption that more reasoning always leads to better LLM decisions, particularly in high-stakes financial applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the effectiveness of large language models (LLMs) in performing zero-shot financial sentiment analysis. It compares several LLMs, including proprietary models from OpenAI (GPT-40, GPT-4.1, o3-mini) and smaller, fine-tuned models (FinBERT-Prosus, FinBERT-Tone), using the Financial PhraseBank dataset. The study focuses on how different prompting strategies, designed to simulate "System 1" (fast, intuitive) and "System 2" (slow, deliberate) thinking, influence the alignment of LLM outputs with human-labeled sentiment. The key finding is that prompting LLMs to engage in explicit reasoning (via Chain-of-Thought or model design) often *reduces* alignment with human sentiment, especially in less ambiguous cases.  The best performance is achieved by GPT-40 without CoT prompting, mirroring System 1. The paper suggests that for financial sentiment classification, a fast, intuitive approach aligns better with human judgment than deliberate reasoning, challenging the conventional assumption that more reasoning always leads to better LLM decisions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in its empirical examination of the effects of different reasoning strategies on a subjective task, financial sentiment analysis. While the general idea of CoT impacting performance isn't entirely new, the *counterintuitive* finding that it *degrades* performance in this specific domain adds a valuable insight.  The paper explicitly connects LLM prompting strategies to cognitive models (System 1 vs. System 2 thinking), which provides a useful framework for understanding the results.  The exploration of how ambiguity and linguistic complexity interact with reasoning is also a novel contribution. Prior works have extensively focused on enhancing reasoning capabilities, but this paper focuses on finding instances when the reasoning capability doesn't help.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its practical implications for deploying LLMs in financial applications.  It cautions against blindly applying reasoning-based approaches and highlights the importance of matching prompting structure to the task's nature. This is particularly relevant in high-stakes financial contexts where alignment with human judgment is crucial for regulatory compliance and effective risk management. By demonstrating that intuitive approaches can outperform deliberative reasoning in specific domains, the paper prompts a more nuanced and task-specific approach to LLM prompting and deployment. The work is also significant as it draws direct parallels between cognitive science with System 1 and System 2 thinking with LLM prompting strategies. This opens up more avenues to explore novel prompting strategies based on known cognitive science principles.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Question:** The paper clearly articulates its research question and focuses on a well-defined problem.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The study uses a standard dataset (Financial PhraseBank) and a variety of LLMs and prompting strategies, providing a comprehensive evaluation. The use of inter-annotator agreement levels as a proxy for ambiguity is a clever approach.
*   **Counterintuitive Finding:** The most compelling aspect of the paper is its unexpected finding that reasoning can *hurt* performance in sentiment analysis. This challenges conventional wisdom and prompts further investigation.
*   **Cognitive Framework:** Linking LLM prompting strategies to System 1 and System 2 thinking provides a valuable framework for understanding the results and generating future research directions.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers practical guidance for deploying LLMs in financial applications, emphasizing the importance of task-specific prompt engineering.
*   **Thorough Analysis:** The analysis goes beyond simple accuracy metrics, exploring the impact of ambiguity, linguistic complexity, and reasoning verbosity.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:** The findings are specific to financial sentiment analysis and might not generalize to other subjective tasks or domains. The Financial PhraseBank dataset, while widely used, has limitations in terms of representativeness.
*   **Proprietary LLMs:** The inclusion of proprietary LLMs (GPT-40, GPT-4.1, o3-mini) limits the reproducibility and transparency of the research. However, this issue is appropriately addressed as it benchmarks the closed-source models to the open-source ones.
*   **The role of fine-tuning:** The work mentions FinBERT-Prosus, which is used as a benchmark. It would have been more relevant to include other few-shot fine-tuning approaches to measure the increase in the model performance and what implications reasoning brings.
*   **Limited exploration of failure cases:** Although the paper discusses the failure cases, these are high-level overviews. Including a thorough analysis of the specific failures in the case study could further reveal areas where reasoning hurts or helps.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence how LLMs are deployed in financial applications and other subjective tasks. It is likely to encourage more task-specific prompt engineering and a more nuanced understanding of the trade-offs between reasoning and alignment with human judgment. The System 1/System 2 framework could inspire further research into cognitive-inspired prompt engineering techniques. The open-sourcing of the data and methodology would give more exposure to this work.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid and well-executed study with a counterintuitive finding that is potentially influential. Its strengths lie in its clear research question, rigorous methodology, and practical implications. The limitations regarding generalizability, proprietary LLMs, and the breadth of financial sentiment topics are acknowledged and don't diminish the paper's core contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Are LLMs Reliable Translators of Logical Reasoning Across Lexically Diversified Contexts?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04575v1)
- **Authors**: Qingchuan Li, Jiatong Li, Zirui Liu, Mingyue Cheng, Yuting Zeng, Qi Liu, Tongxuan Liu
- **Abstract**: Neuro-symbolic approaches combining large language models (LLMs) with solvers excels in logical reasoning problems need long reasoning chains. In this paradigm, LLMs serve as translators, converting natural language reasoning problems into formal logic formulas. Then reliable symbolic solvers return correct solutions. Despite their success, we find that LLMs, as translators, struggle to handle lexical diversification, a common linguistic phenomenon, indicating that LLMs as logic translators are unreliable in real-world scenarios. Moreover, existing logical reasoning benchmarks lack lexical diversity, failing to challenge LLMs' ability to translate such text and thus obscuring this issue. In this work, we propose SCALe, a benchmark designed to address this significant gap through **logic-invariant lexical diversification**. By using LLMs to transform original benchmark datasets into lexically diversified but logically equivalent versions, we evaluate LLMs' ability to consistently map diverse expressions to uniform logical symbols on these new datasets. Experiments using SCALe further confirm that current LLMs exhibit deficiencies in this capability. Building directly on the deficiencies identified through our benchmark, we propose a new method, MenTaL, to address this limitation. This method guides LLMs to first construct a table unifying diverse expressions before performing translation. Applying MenTaL through in-context learning and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) significantly improves the performance of LLM translators on lexically diversified text. Our code is now available at https://github.com/wufeiwuwoshihua/LexicalDiver.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Are LLMs Reliable Translators of Logical Reasoning Across Lexically Diversified Contexts?":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a critical weakness in the current neuro-symbolic approach to logical reasoning, where Large Language Models (LLMs) act as translators between natural language and formal logic.  The authors find that LLMs struggle with *lexical diversification* – the natural variation in how concepts are expressed in language – and that current benchmarks don't adequately challenge this ability.  To address this, they introduce **SCALE**, a novel benchmark specifically designed to evaluate LLMs' "Semantic Consistency Mapping Ability with Logical-invariant Lexical Diversification." SCALE uses LLMs to transform existing datasets into lexically diverse but logically equivalent versions. Experiments using SCALE reveal deficiencies in existing LLMs' translation accuracy in such diverse contexts.  Building on this, the authors propose **MenTaL**, a method where LLMs first build a mental representation table unifying diverse expressions before translating to logic. MenTaL improves LLM performance significantly, both through in-context learning and supervised fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in identifying and formalizing the problem of lexical diversification in the context of LLM-based logical reasoning. While adversarial text attacks and techniques for improving LLM robustness exist, this paper focuses on a specific type of linguistic variation that's particularly relevant to neuro-symbolic systems. Creating SCALE, with its method for generating logically equivalent but lexically diversified datasets, is a significant contribution. The MenTaL method is less novel, drawing inspiration from cognitive science theories of mental representation, but its application to the problem is well-motivated.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant for several reasons:

    *   It highlights a real-world limitation of current LLMs in logical reasoning applications. Lexical diversification is pervasive in natural language, and LLMs' inability to handle it reliably hinders their usability in practical settings.
    *   It challenges the validity of current logical reasoning benchmarks, which tend to oversimplify linguistic variation and thus may overestimate LLM capabilities.
    *   It provides a concrete benchmark (SCALE) to evaluate and address this limitation, promoting more robust LLM-based reasoning systems.
    *   It proposes and validates a method (MenTaL) to mitigate the problem, offering a practical solution.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   Rigorous methodology for creating SCALE.
    *   Empirical validation demonstrating the issue and the effectiveness of MenTaL.
    *   Analysis of error types.
    *   Open-source release of SCALE and MenTaL for future research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis of why LLMs struggle with lexical diversification. Is it primarily a matter of training data bias, architectural limitations, or something else?
    *   The lexical diversification methods within SCALE are not entirely controllable. The curated strategy works in narrow settings, and the dynamic strategy relies on potentially imperfect LLM-based synonym generation.
    *   The focus is mainly on simple logical reasoning tasks. It is important to explore if these limitations affect more complex logical reasoning in various domain.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence the field in several ways:

    *   Researchers will likely use SCALE to evaluate and improve the robustness of LLMs in logical reasoning tasks.
    *   The paper may encourage the development of more linguistically diverse benchmarks for other NLP tasks.
    *   The MenTaL method could inspire other techniques for incorporating cognitive principles into LLM design.
    *   The paper's focus on the translation problem within neuro-symbolic systems will hopefully encourage further attention in this research area.
* **Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

    The paper is not a groundbreaking theoretical advancement. However, it makes a substantial contribution by pinpointing a crucial, overlooked problem (lexical diversification) in LLM-based logical reasoning, providing a solid benchmark to evaluate progress, and offering a pragmatic method for enhancement. While there is room for improvement in the diversification methods and in a deeper theoretical understanding, the paper is well-executed and fills a significant gap in the field. The work is likely to drive further research and development of more reliable LLMs for logical reasoning.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Selecting Demonstrations for Many-Shot In-Context Learning via Gradient Matching
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04579v1)
- **Authors**: Jianfei Zhang, Bei Li, Jun Bai, Rumei Li, Yanmeng Wang, Chenghua Lin, Wenge Rong
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) empowers Large Language Models (LLMs) for rapid task adaptation without Fine-Tuning (FT), but its reliance on demonstration selection remains a critical challenge. While many-shot ICL shows promising performance through scaled demonstrations, the selection method for many-shot demonstrations remains limited to random selection in existing work. Since the conventional instance-level retrieval is not suitable for many-shot scenarios, we hypothesize that the data requirements for in-context learning and fine-tuning are analogous. To this end, we introduce a novel gradient matching approach that selects demonstrations by aligning fine-tuning gradients between the entire training set of the target task and the selected examples, so as to approach the learning effect on the entire training set within the selected examples. Through gradient matching on relatively small models, e.g., Qwen2.5-3B or Llama3-8B, our method consistently outperforms random selection on larger LLMs from 4-shot to 128-shot scenarios across 9 diverse datasets. For instance, it surpasses random selection by 4% on Qwen2.5-72B and Llama3-70B, and by around 2% on 5 closed-source LLMs. This work unlocks more reliable and effective many-shot ICL, paving the way for its broader application.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the problem of demonstration selection in many-shot in-context learning (ICL) for large language models (LLMs). Unlike few-shot ICL where demonstrations are carefully selected, many-shot ICL often relies on random selection due to scalability concerns. The authors propose a "Curriculum Latent Gradient" (CLG) method.  CLG selects demonstrations by aligning the fine-tuning gradients of the entire training set with the gradients of the selected examples, aiming to mimic the full training set's learning dynamics within the selected subset. Experiments across diverse NLP tasks and LLMs (including both open-source and closed-source) demonstrate that CLG consistently outperforms random selection and other baseline methods, often by a significant margin. The authors also investigate the transferability of selected demonstrations, the relationship between fine-tuning and ICL performance, and the diversity/coverage of the selected demonstrations.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using gradient matching to select demonstrations is novel and well-motivated. The insight that ICL and fine-tuning have analogous data requirements is a significant conceptual contribution. CLG offers a different perspective from instance-based or diversity-based demonstration selection. The "curriculum" aspect (using gradients across multiple training epochs) adds a temporal dimension to the selection process, which is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a practically relevant problem.  Many-shot ICL is becoming increasingly important as context windows expand, and this work offers a viable alternative to the default random selection strategy. By significantly improving ICL performance, the paper contributes to more efficient and effective usage of LLMs.  Demonstrating transferability to closed-source LLMs is particularly significant as it highlights the method's applicability in real-world scenarios where model weights are inaccessible.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Motivated:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches and the potential benefits of gradient matching.
    *   **Solid Experimental Design:** The authors conduct extensive experiments across a wide range of datasets, tasks, and LLMs. The use of both open-source and closed-source models enhances the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper not only reports performance gains but also provides insightful analyses, including ablation studies, investigations of diversity and coverage, and the relationship between FT and ICL.
    *   **Practicality:** The paper discusses the computational cost of CLG and demonstrates its efficiency even at scale.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Gradient Approximation:**  The method relies on approximating the true ICL learning dynamics using gradients from a smaller model's fine-tuning process. This approximation may not always be accurate, especially if the smaller model has significantly different inductive biases than the larger model. The authors do acknowledge this implicit assumption.
    *   **Algorithm Complexity:** While claiming efficiency, calculating and matching gradients can be computationally intensive, especially for very large training sets. The authors could further refine and reduce the computational complexity of CLG.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Order Sensitivity:** The paper acknowledges the order sensitivity limitation of ICL. Although not a focus of the paper, there's a missed opportunity to incorporate order considerations as a minor element given the curriculum-based aspect of CLG. This could add another layer of refinement in demonstration selection.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence how demonstrations are selected for many-shot ICL. The CLG method is relatively simple to implement and could be incorporated into existing ICL pipelines.  The insights about the relationship between fine-tuning and ICL can also guide future research in this area. The demonstration of transferability across various LLMs makes the method broadly applicable.

*   **Justification for the Score:** The paper presents a novel and well-supported approach to a practically relevant problem. While the gradient matching relies on an approximation and the computational complexity should be kept in mind, the empirical results and analysis demonstrate clear benefits over existing methods. It introduces a fresh perspective and offers practical guidance for better leveraging many-shot ICL. The results have the potential to be reused and incorporated across multiple LLM models and use cases.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### LESS: Large Language Model Enhanced Semi-Supervised Learning for Speech Foundational Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04586v1)
- **Authors**: Wen Ding, Fan Qian
- **Abstract**: We introduce LESS (Large Language Model Enhanced Semi-supervised Learning), a versatile framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to correct pseudo labels generated from in-the-wild data. Within the LESS framework, pseudo-labeled text from Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) or Automatic Speech Translation (AST) of the unsupervised data is refined by an LLM, and augmented by a data filtering strategy to optimize LLM knowledge transfer efficiency. Experiments on both Mandarin ASR and Spanish-to-English AST tasks show that LESS achieves a notable absolute WER reduction of 3.77% on the Wenet Speech test set, as well as BLEU scores of 34.0 and 64.7 on Callhome and Fisher test sets respectively. These results validate the adaptability of LESS across different languages, tasks, and domains. Ablation studies conducted with various LLMs and prompt configurations provide novel insights into leveraging LLM-derived knowledge for speech processing applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LESS (Large Language Model Enhanced Semi-supervised Learning), a framework designed to improve speech foundational model (SFM) performance on ASR and AST tasks by leveraging LLMs to refine pseudo-labels generated from in-the-wild, unlabeled data. The framework incorporates a data filtering strategy to optimize the transfer of knowledge from the LLM. Experiments on Mandarin ASR and Spanish-to-English AST demonstrate significant WER and BLEU score improvements. Ablation studies explore different LLMs and prompt configurations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to refine pseudo-labels for SFMs is reasonably novel. While previous work has explored pseudo-labeling and LLM-assisted correction, the paper focuses on applying this in a semi-supervised setting for *large-scale* SFMs and using *in-the-wild* data, which presents different challenges compared to curated datasets. The data filtering strategy is also a potentially valuable contribution, helping to focus the benefits of the LLM correction. The investigation of different LLMs and prompts is also a useful contribution, providing insights on how to effectively apply LLMs for speech processing tasks.

*   **Significance:** The results demonstrate tangible improvements in ASR and AST performance, particularly on out-of-domain datasets. This suggests the potential for LESS to enhance the robustness and generalization of SFMs. The detailed experimental analysis, including ablation studies, provides valuable practical guidelines for researchers and practitioners working with SFMs and semi-supervised learning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   Comprehensive experimental setup, including different languages, tasks, and datasets.
    *   Detailed ablation studies that offer valuable insights into the effectiveness of different LLMs and prompt configurations.
    *   The practical results show significant improvement to ASR and AST.
    *   The work is reproducible and the authors plan to release the recipes.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The individual components (pseudo-labeling, LLM correction, data filtering) are not entirely new. The novelty lies in their specific combination and application to large-scale SFMs with in-the-wild data.
    *   The experimental setup focuses on Whisper-Large-v3, Yi-Large, Qwen and LLama 3, but more experiments on a broader range of SFMs may be necessary to claim a wide generalisation of the approach.
    *   While the ablation studies are informative, they could be expanded to investigate more parameters, such as different filtering thresholds or LLM training strategies.
    *   The results are not compared against several contemporary methods in the semi-supervised speech processing space.

*   **Overall Impact:** The paper offers a practical and effective method for improving SFM performance, particularly in low-resource scenarios where high-quality labeled data is scarce. The insights from the ablation studies could guide future research on leveraging LLMs for speech processing.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a practical and well-executed approach to semi-supervised learning for speech foundational models. While it builds upon existing techniques, the specific combination of pseudo-labeling, LLM-based refinement, and data filtering, along with the focus on large-scale models and in-the-wild data, constitutes a valuable contribution. The experimental results are convincing, and the ablation studies provide useful insights. However, the reliance on a small number of SFMs and lack of comparison against contemporary methods in the field somewhat limit its overall impact. Therefore, the paper merits a score of 7.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Safe: Enhancing Mathematical Reasoning in Large Language Models via Retrospective Step-aware Formal Verification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04592v1)
- **Authors**: Chengwu Liu, Ye Yuan, Yichun Yin, Yan Xu, Xin Xu, Zaoyu Chen, Yasheng Wang, Lifeng Shang, Qun Liu, Ming Zhang
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting has become the de facto method to elicit reasoning capabilities from large language models (LLMs). However, to mitigate hallucinations in CoT that are notoriously difficult to detect, current methods such as process reward models (PRMs) or self-consistency operate as opaque boxes and do not provide checkable evidence for their judgments, possibly limiting their effectiveness. To address this issue, we draw inspiration from the idea that "the gold standard for supporting a mathematical claim is to provide a proof". We propose a retrospective, step-aware formal verification framework $Safe$. Rather than assigning arbitrary scores, we strive to articulate mathematical claims in formal mathematical language Lean 4 at each reasoning step and provide formal proofs to identify hallucinations. We evaluate our framework $Safe$ across multiple language models and various mathematical datasets, demonstrating a significant performance improvement while offering interpretable and verifiable evidence. We also propose $FormalStep$ as a benchmark for step correctness theorem proving with $30,809$ formal statements. To the best of our knowledge, our work represents the first endeavor to utilize formal mathematical language Lean 4 for verifying natural language content generated by LLMs, aligning with the reason why formal mathematical languages were created in the first place: to provide a robust foundation for hallucination-prone human-written proofs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Safe: Enhancing Mathematical Reasoning in Large Language Models via Retrospective Step-aware Formal Verification":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Safe, a novel framework designed to enhance the mathematical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by employing retrospective step-aware formal verification.  Instead of relying solely on opaque evaluation methods like Process Reward Models (PRMs) or self-consistency, Safe leverages the formal mathematical language Lean 4 to articulate and formally verify mathematical claims at each step of the LLM's reasoning process. The framework decomposes complex reasoning trajectories into simpler steps, auto-formalizes each step into Lean 4 statements, and then uses automated theorem provers to identify hallucinations. A new benchmark dataset, FormalStep, consisting of formal statements designed for step correctness theorem proving, is introduced. The retrospective formal verification scores are integrated with prospective scores from PRMs to improve performance. The study demonstrates significant performance improvements across multiple language models and datasets, offering verifiable and interpretable evidence of correctness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The most significant novelty lies in the use of a formal mathematical language (Lean 4) *specifically* to verify *individual reasoning steps* generated by LLMs in a retrospective manner. While auto-formalization and automated theorem proving are existing areas of research, their application in this fine-grained, retrospective verification loop is a unique contribution. The creation of FormalStep, a dataset focused on step-wise verification rather than full problem formalization, also adds to the novelty. Distinguishing between prospective and retrospective verification is also helpful, clarifying the approach relative to prior art.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a critical challenge in LLM research: the difficulty of detecting hallucinations in chain-of-thought reasoning.  By providing a formal, verifiable method for checking the correctness of each reasoning step, Safe offers a potentially more reliable approach than existing methods. The interpretable nature of the formal proofs also enhances trust and transparency.  The improved performance demonstrated on multiple datasets suggests a real-world impact.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong methodological contribution:** The framework is well-defined and technically sound.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The paper provides comprehensive experimental results on a variety of datasets and language models.
    *   **Interpretability:** The use of formal verification provides inherently interpretable evidence.
    *   **Clear distinction from existing methods:**  The paper clearly positions its approach relative to PRMs and self-consistency techniques.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational cost:** Formal verification is inherently more computationally expensive than using PRMs or self-consistency.  This cost could limit the scalability of the approach.  The paper acknowledges this but could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the practical limitations of the approach due to compute costs. While the paper says on average approximately 1.02 automation formalism attempts and 6.97 automation theorem proving, the increased query to an LLM is approximately 4 to 8x greater than P RMs and the authors need to further reduce and provide a detail plan on doing so.
    *   **Dependency on Auto-Formalization Quality:** The accuracy of Safe depends heavily on the ability of LLMs to reliably auto-formalize natural language reasoning steps.  Failures in auto-formalization can lead to false negatives. Also, because the theorems were obtained through auto formalisation, they may not be necessary provable.
    *   **Limited scope of Lean 4:** Lean 4, while powerful, may not be suitable for all types of mathematical reasoning. The paper admits this limitation regarding geometry and combinatorics.
    *   **Evaluation based on Correctness of Answer only:** The evaluation only consider the correctness of the answers and do not address the fluency or naturalness of the reasoning steps.
    *   **State Aggregator:** The paper doesn't provide details on their training set and the hyperparameters of the algorithm, thus it will be difficult to reproduce their work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The Safe framework has the potential to influence the development of more reliable and trustworthy LLMs for mathematical reasoning and other domains requiring high accuracy. The emphasis on verifiable reasoning steps could become a standard practice. Furthermore, the FormalStep dataset can spur further research in auto-formalization and automated theorem proving in LLM-generated content.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field by introducing a formal verification framework for enhancing mathematical reasoning in LLMs. The use of Lean 4 for step-wise verification is innovative, and the empirical results demonstrate promising performance improvements. The clear differentiation from existing techniques and the emphasis on interpretable evidence are valuable. However, the computational cost, dependence on auto-formalization quality, and limitations of Lean 4 restrict the score. The lack of details on state aggregator and not addressing the fluency or naturalness of the reasoning steps impact the overall scope of its significance. While Safe is a promising approach, further research is needed to address these limitations and fully realize its potential.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A MISMATCHED Benchmark for Scientific Natural Language Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04603v1)
- **Authors**: Firoz Shaik, Mobashir Sadat, Nikita Gautam, Doina Caragea, Cornelia Caragea
- **Abstract**: Scientific Natural Language Inference (NLI) is the task of predicting the semantic relation between a pair of sentences extracted from research articles. Existing datasets for this task are derived from various computer science (CS) domains, whereas non-CS domains are completely ignored. In this paper, we introduce a novel evaluation benchmark for scientific NLI, called MISMATCHED. The new MISMATCHED benchmark covers three non-CS domains-PSYCHOLOGY, ENGINEERING, and PUBLIC HEALTH, and contains 2,700 human annotated sentence pairs. We establish strong baselines on MISMATCHED using both Pre-trained Small Language Models (SLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs). Our best performing baseline shows a Macro F1 of only 78.17% illustrating the substantial headroom for future improvements. In addition to introducing the MISMATCHED benchmark, we show that incorporating sentence pairs having an implicit scientific NLI relation between them in model training improves their performance on scientific NLI. We make our dataset and code publicly available on GitHub.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new evaluation benchmark called MISMATCHED for scientific Natural Language Inference (NLI).  The benchmark focuses on scientific domains *outside* of computer science (specifically, Psychology, Engineering, and Public Health), which are underrepresented in existing scientific NLI datasets.  The authors construct the MISMATCHED dataset, establish strong baselines using both small language models (SLMs) and large language models (LLMs), and show that performance is substantially lower than on in-domain data, indicating a challenging benchmark for out-of-domain NLI evaluation. The paper also explores incorporating sentence pairs with *implicit* relationships (adjacent sentences without explicit linking phrases) into model training, showing that this can improve performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the creation of the MISMATCHED dataset, filling a gap in the existing scientific NLI landscape by explicitly targeting non-CS domains. The exploration of implicit relationships, while not entirely new in the general NLI space, is a valuable addition in the scientific context. However, the methodology for creating the training data using distant supervision is similar to previous works and therefore has less novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the following:

    *   **Addressing Domain Shift:**  Highlighting and addressing the issue of domain shift in scientific NLI is important.  Models trained and evaluated primarily on CS data may not generalize well to other scientific disciplines.  The MISMATCHED dataset provides a concrete way to measure and mitigate this.
    *   **Resource Creation:**  The creation of a new, publicly available dataset (MISMATCHED) is a tangible contribution to the research community, enabling further work in the area.
    *   **Practical Implications:**  Scientific NLI has the potential to improve downstream tasks like topic classification and citation intent classification across various scientific disciplines. Enhancing the robustness and generalizability of NLI models is therefore of significant practical value.
    * **Implicit relationships:** It can be inferred that including implicit relationships in data training improves the general performance of the data.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper identifies and articulates a clear and well-motivated problem (lack of non-CS data in scientific NLI).
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The data collection, annotation, and baseline experiments are well-executed.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper provides empirical evidence to support its claims, with thorough experimental results.
    *   **Open Resource:** Making the dataset and code publicly available is a major strength, promoting reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The MISMATCHED dataset covers only three non-CS domains. While this is a valuable start, further expansion to other scientific areas would be beneficial. The limited set of non-CS domains is probably the main weakness.
    *   **Baseline Limitations:** While the baselines are well-established, more sophisticated domain adaptation techniques could have been explored to further analyze the challenges of the dataset.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Errors:** The paper provides some high-level performance results and the confusion matrix. A deeper analysis of the types of errors made by the models on the MISMATCHED dataset would be valuable in identifying specific challenges and areas for future research.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in scientific NLI by:

    *   Encouraging the development of more robust and generalizable models that are less sensitive to domain shift.
    *   Motivating the creation of additional datasets in other scientific domains.
    *   Providing a benchmark for evaluating domain adaptation techniques for scientific NLI.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper makes a definite, although not groundbreaking, contribution to the field of scientific NLI. It identifies and addresses a specific gap in the existing literature, provides a valuable new resource (the MISMATCHED dataset), and presents empirical evidence to support its claims. The novelty of the methodology is limited (applying existing techniques to a new domain), and further analysis of the data and error types would have been beneficial. Because of the limited sets of domains and the limited methodology improvements, it does not have the reach of a great paper.
