- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### Membership Inference Attacks on Sequence Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05126v1)
- **Authors**: Lorenzo Rossi, Michael Aerni, Jie Zhang, Florian Tramèr
- **Abstract**: Sequence models, such as Large Language Models (LLMs) and autoregressive image generators, have a tendency to memorize and inadvertently leak sensitive information. While this tendency has critical legal implications, existing tools are insufficient to audit the resulting risks. We hypothesize that those tools' shortcomings are due to mismatched assumptions. Thus, we argue that effectively measuring privacy leakage in sequence models requires leveraging the correlations inherent in sequential generation. To illustrate this, we adapt a state-of-the-art membership inference attack to explicitly model within-sequence correlations, thereby demonstrating how a strong existing attack can be naturally extended to suit the structure of sequence models. Through a case study, we show that our adaptations consistently improve the effectiveness of memorization audits without introducing additional computational costs. Our work hence serves as an important stepping stone toward reliable memorization audits for large sequence models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of existing membership inference attacks (MIAs) when applied to sequence models, such as Large Language Models (LLMs). It argues that standard MIAs, like the Likelihood Ratio Attack (LiRA), fail to effectively detect memorization in sequence models because they treat sequence elements as independent, ignoring the inherent correlations in sequential generation.  The authors adapt LiRA to explicitly model within-sequence correlations by estimating the covariance between sequence elements.  They demonstrate that this adaptation significantly improves the effectiveness of MIAs without introducing additional computational costs. The paper evaluates these adaptations across different sequence model architectures (LSTM, Pythia) and canary types (average-case, worst-case), demonstrating consistent improvements in detecting memorization. Finally, they also explore which tokens are most important to the attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The key novelty lies in recognizing and addressing the mismatched assumptions of standard MIAs when applied to sequence models. While LiRA itself is not a novel attack, the *adaptation* of LiRA to incorporate within-sequence correlations *is* a novel contribution. The use of covariance estimation (especially with techniques like OAS) to capture these correlations is a technically sound and practically relevant approach. The insight that treating sequence elements independently is a flaw in existing MIAs for sequence models is a valuable observation. However, It could be argued that accounting for token-level correlation is a relatively intuitive modification to LiRA, which diminishes the novelty factor slightly.

**Significance:** The paper is significant because it offers a practical method for improving the reliability of memorization audits in large sequence models.  Given the growing concerns about privacy risks associated with LLMs and similar models, more effective MIAs are crucial. By showing how to adapt a state-of-the-art attack to better suit the structure of sequence models, the paper provides a concrete stepping stone towards more robust privacy evaluations. The empirical results, demonstrating consistent improvements across different models and settings, strengthens the paper's significance. The careful investigation into the impact of the number of shadow models, and the comparison of covariance estimation techniques, adds to the paper's practical value. Furthermore, the discovery of worst-case examples of potential memorization is interesting in practice.

**Strengths:**
*   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the problem with existing MIAs in the context of sequence models.
*   **Technically Sound Approach:** The adaptation of LiRA using covariance estimation is well-justified and technically well-executed.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The empirical evaluation is thorough, covering different model architectures, canary types, and settings.
*   **Practical Relevance:**  The paper provides practical recommendations for improving MIAs in sequence models, including the use of efficient covariance estimation techniques like OAS.
*   **Well-written and Well-Structured:** The paper is clear, concise, and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Contribution:** While valuable, the adaptation of LiRA might be considered an incremental improvement rather than a radical breakthrough. Although, it is worth noting that the paper doesn't propose an entirely novel attack, and it mainly builds upon previously existing work.
*   **Limited Scope:** The study primarily focuses on one type of MIA (LiRA). Investigating whether other MIAs benefit from the same type of adaptations would further strengthen the paper.
*   **Computational cost:** The paper doesn't fully address the computational cost associated with covariance estimation, especially for very long sequences. While OAS helps, calculating covariances for sequence elements could still be expensive. It is worth noting that computational cost is a main motivation for proposing a good adaptation.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

*   The paper makes a valuable and practically relevant contribution by addressing the shortcomings of standard MIAs when applied to sequence models. The approach of explicitly modeling within-sequence correlations is well-justified and empirically supported.
*   The novelty, while significant in its specific context, is somewhat limited by the incremental nature of adapting an existing attack. And perhaps, considering token correlation is intuitively appealing and might not be surprising.
*   The comprehensive evaluation strengthens the paper's value and provides practical guidance for improving MIAs in sequence models.
*   The limitations, such as the limited scope (focus on LiRA) and the incomplete discussion of computational costs, detract slightly from the paper's overall impact.
*   In conclusion, the paper offers a solid contribution that advances the field of privacy auditing for sequence models and deserves recognition for its practical relevance and insightful analysis.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### PixCell: A generative foundation model for digital histopathology images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05127v1)
- **Authors**: Srikar Yellapragada, Alexandros Graikos, Zilinghan Li, Kostas Triaridis, Varun Belagali, Saarthak Kapse, Tarak Nath Nandi, Ravi K Madduri, Prateek Prasanna, Tahsin Kurc, Rajarsi R. Gupta, Joel Saltz, Dimitris Samaras
- **Abstract**: The digitization of histology slides has revolutionized pathology, providing massive datasets for cancer diagnosis and research. Contrastive self-supervised and vision-language models have been shown to effectively mine large pathology datasets to learn discriminative representations. On the other hand, generative models, capable of synthesizing realistic and diverse images, present a compelling solution to address unique problems in pathology that involve synthesizing images; overcoming annotated data scarcity, enabling privacy-preserving data sharing, and performing inherently generative tasks, such as virtual staining. We introduce PixCell, the first diffusion-based generative foundation model for histopathology. We train PixCell on PanCan-30M, a vast, diverse dataset derived from 69,184 H\&E-stained whole slide images covering various cancer types. We employ a progressive training strategy and a self-supervision-based conditioning that allows us to scale up training without any annotated data. PixCell generates diverse and high-quality images across multiple cancer types, which we find can be used in place of real data to train a self-supervised discriminative model. Synthetic images shared between institutions are subject to fewer regulatory barriers than would be the case with real clinical images. Furthermore, we showcase the ability to precisely control image generation using a small set of annotated images, which can be used for both data augmentation and educational purposes. Testing on a cell segmentation task, a mask-guided PixCell enables targeted data augmentation, improving downstream performance. Finally, we demonstrate PixCell's ability to use H\&E structural staining to infer results from molecular marker studies; we use this capability to infer IHC staining from H\&E images. Our trained models are publicly released to accelerate research in computational pathology.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the PixCell paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PixCell, the first diffusion-based generative foundation model for digital histopathology images. Trained on PanCan-30M, a large and diverse dataset of H&E-stained whole slide images, PixCell generates high-quality, diverse histopathology images. The paper demonstrates the model's ability to: (1) be used to train self-supervised learning (SSL) models, effectively replacing real data and addressing privacy concerns; (2) enable controllable image generation through conditioning on cell segmentation masks, facilitating data augmentation; and (3) perform stain translation from H&E to IHC images, achieving state-of-the-art results. The model weights and code are publicly released.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of a large-scale generative foundation model specifically for histopathology. While diffusion models have been used in other domains, their application to histopathology at this scale and with the demonstrated capabilities (SSL training, controllable generation, and stain translation) is a significant contribution. The use of UNI-2h embeddings for conditioning is also a clever adaptation to the lack of image-caption pairs. The progressive training strategy and the use of a large, diverse, pan-cancer dataset are essential for scaling up the model.

*   **Significance:** The paper has potentially high significance.
    *   **Data Scarcity and Privacy:** Generative models address data scarcity and privacy issues in pathology, allowing for data sharing without regulatory hurdles. The demonstration that synthetic images can effectively train SSL models is a crucial step.
    *   **Controllable Generation:** The ability to control image generation based on cell masks opens avenues for targeted data augmentation and educational tools.
    *   **Stain Translation:** Virtual staining could significantly reduce the need for expensive and time-consuming IHC procedures.
    *   **Foundation Model:** PixCell offers a foundation for future research in computational pathology.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Scale and Diversity:** The PanCan-30M dataset is substantial, contributing to the model's generalizability.
    *   **Multiple Applications:** The paper convincingly demonstrates PixCell's versatility through multiple applications.
    *   **State-of-the-art Results:** The stain translation achieves SoTA, further validating the approach.
    *   **Open Release:** The public release of code and models promotes further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Lack of Pixel-Perfect Alignment in H&E to IHC Translation:** The model operates patch-wise due to dataset imperfections which results in sub-optimal stain translation.
    *   **Generalization Limits of IHC Translation:** While good results are shown on MIST-HER2, the performance and robustness on other IHC stains require further evaluation. The reliance on a rectification flow model, while computationally efficient, might limit the complexity of the learned transformation.
    *   **Data Privacy:** The paper acknowledges limitations in data privacy.
    *   **Limited evaluation for downstream tasks using the synthesized data:** Although the paper evaluates synthetic images by training SSL models using the synthesized data, it is worth evaluating whether other downstream tasks like classification, segmentation are also performing comparably.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to stimulate significant follow-up research in generative models for histopathology. It establishes a strong baseline and provides a blueprint for future work. Researchers can build upon PixCell for more advanced controllable generation, stain translation, and synthetic data augmentation techniques.

**Overall:**

While there are some limitations, the paper represents a substantial advancement in computational pathology. The scale of the model, the diversity of the training data, and the demonstration of multiple impactful applications make it a significant contribution. The weaknesses, primarily related to dataset limitations and the need for more comprehensive evaluation, do not detract significantly from the overall impact. The publicly released models and code will undoubtedly accelerate research in this area.

### DiCoRe: Enhancing Zero-shot Event Detection via Divergent-Convergent LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05128v1)
- **Authors**: Tanmay Parekh, Kartik Mehta, Ninareh Mehrabi, Kai-Wei Chang, Nanyun Peng
- **Abstract**: Zero-shot Event Detection (ED), the task of identifying event mentions in natural language text without any training data, is critical for document understanding in specialized domains. Understanding the complex event ontology, extracting domain-specific triggers from the passage, and structuring them appropriately overloads and limits the utility of Large Language Models (LLMs) for zero-shot ED. To this end, we propose DiCoRe, a divergent-convergent reasoning framework that decouples the task of ED using Dreamer and Grounder. Dreamer encourages divergent reasoning through open-ended event discovery, which helps to boost event coverage. Conversely, Grounder introduces convergent reasoning to align the free-form predictions with the task-specific instructions using finite-state machine guided constrained decoding. Additionally, an LLM-Judge verifies the final outputs to ensure high precision. Through extensive experiments on six datasets across five domains and nine LLMs, we demonstrate how DiCoRe consistently outperforms prior zero-shot, transfer-learning, and reasoning baselines, achieving 4-7% average F1 gains over the best baseline -- establishing DiCoRe as a strong zero-shot ED framework.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the paper "DiCoRe: Enhancing Zero-shot Event Detection via Divergent-Convergent LLM Reasoning":

**Summary:**
The paper introduces DICORE, a novel framework to improve zero-shot event detection (ED) performance using Large Language Models (LLMs). DICORE addresses limitations in directly prompting LLMs for ED due to the complexity of event ontologies and task constraints. It employs a divergent-convergent reasoning approach, decoupling the ED task into two main components: Dreamer and Grounder. Dreamer fosters divergent reasoning by enabling open-ended event discovery without task-specific constraints. Grounder then introduces convergent reasoning to align Dreamer's predictions with a closed event ontology, utilizing a finite-state machine (FSM) for constrained decoding. Finally, an LLM Judge verifies the grounded predictions. The paper demonstrates through experiments on six datasets that DICORE consistently outperforms existing zero-shot, transfer learning, and reasoning baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core innovation of this paper is the separation of the event detection task into divergent and convergent reasoning stages, each optimized for specific aspects of the problem. While the individual components (like FSM-guided decoding and LLM-based judging) have been explored in other contexts, their specific combination and application to zero-shot ED in the DICORE pipeline appear novel. The idea of using a "Dreamer" to generate potentially relevant events without constraints to increase recall is a compelling approach to overcome inherent biases and limitations of LLMs.
*   **Significance:** Event detection is a crucial task in many downstream applications. The limitations of using LLMs directly for this task and the high cost of expert-annotated data for training motivate research in zero-shot methods. DICORE's improved performance over baselines suggests it can make ED systems more robust and accessible, particularly for specialized domains where labeled data is scarce. The paper also demonstrates a potential pathway to more effective utilization of LLMs for complex tasks by carefully decoupling constraints and leveraging their reasoning capabilities in a structured manner.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation across diverse datasets and LLMs. The consistent outperformance of DICORE over various baselines strengthens the claim that it is a robust zero-shot ED framework.
    *   **Clear Architecture:** The description of DICORE's components and their roles is clear and well-motivated. The paper effectively explains how each part contributes to overall performance.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study provides insights into the contributions of each component of the DICORE pipeline, which helps to confirm the design choices and explain the mechanism of performance gains.
    *   **Efficiency Gains:** Results showing improved F1 scores at a fraction of the computational cost relative to chain-of-thought methods are significant.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The overall system involves multiple components, which could increase the complexity of implementation. The paper is clear but may be a little challenging to reproduce.
    *   **Lack of comparison against LLM-finetuned models directly:** The paper compares against transfer learning models, but could also provide a more direct comparison with LLMs that are finetuned. This would help emphasize the efficiency advantages of the proposed approach.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    DICORE can influence future research in zero-shot ED by demonstrating the effectiveness of divergent-convergent reasoning frameworks. Its design can be adapted for other complex NLP tasks where constraints can hinder LLM performance. The FSM-guided decoding and LLM-Judge components can be incorporated into other IE pipelines. The work also provides valuable insights into the limitations of current LLM prompting strategies for complex IE tasks.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:** While the individual components of DICORE are not entirely novel, their synergistic combination within a carefully designed pipeline for zero-shot ED is a valuable contribution. The paper's strengths lie in its strong empirical results, clear explanations, and demonstration of efficiency gains. The separation of tasks in the proposed framework, however, may increase implementation challenges. Furthermore, the paper lacks a direct comparison with standardly fine-tuned LLMs. Nevertheless, DICORE presents a novel and effective approach that pushes the boundaries of zero-shot learning with LLMs in ED and other IE applications. The observed improvements over existing baselines, along with the conceptual novelty of the method, justify a score of 8, indicating a strong contribution to the field with significant potential for further research and practical applications.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Do Large Language Models Judge Error Severity Like Humans?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05142v1)
- **Authors**: Diege Sun, Guanyi Chen, Fan Zhao, Xiaorong Cheng, Tingting He
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as automated evaluators in natural language generation, yet it remains unclear whether they can accurately replicate human judgments of error severity. In this study, we systematically compare human and LLM assessments of image descriptions containing controlled semantic errors. We extend the experimental framework of van Miltenburg et al. (2020) to both unimodal (text-only) and multimodal (text + image) settings, evaluating four error types: age, gender, clothing type, and clothing colour. Our findings reveal that humans assign varying levels of severity to different error types, with visual context significantly amplifying perceived severity for colour and type errors. Notably, most LLMs assign low scores to gender errors but disproportionately high scores to colour errors, unlike humans, who judge both as highly severe but for different reasons. This suggests that these models may have internalised social norms influencing gender judgments but lack the perceptual grounding to emulate human sensitivity to colour, which is shaped by distinct neural mechanisms. Only one of the evaluated LLMs, Doubao, replicates the human-like ranking of error severity, but it fails to distinguish between error types as clearly as humans. Surprisingly, DeepSeek-V3, a unimodal LLM, achieves the highest alignment with human judgments across both unimodal and multimodal conditions, outperforming even state-of-the-art multimodal models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how well Large Language Models (LLMs) can judge the severity of errors in image descriptions, comparing their assessments to those of human evaluators. It extends previous work by examining both unimodal (text-only) and multimodal (text + image) settings and focusing on different error types (age, gender, clothing type, and clothing colour). The key findings reveal discrepancies between human and LLM judgments, particularly regarding gender and colour errors. Humans are more sensitive to colour errors due to perceptual grounding, while LLMs show biases reflecting social norms in gender judgments. Doubao is the only LLM that replicates the human severity ranking in the unimodal condition but struggles with distinguishing the severity of the errors. Surprisingly, DeepSeek-V3, a unimodal model, achieves a higher alignment with human judgements than many multimodal models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * The paper addresses a relevant and timely question regarding the reliability of LLMs as evaluators in natural language generation (NLG).
    * The experimental design is well-controlled, extending existing methodologies in a systematic way.
    * The comparison of unimodal and multimodal LLMs and conditions is a novel contribution, offering insights into how visual context impacts error assessment.
    * The error types considered (age, gender, clothing type, and clothing colour) are relevant and contribute to understanding biases in LLM evaluation.
    * The analysis is thorough, employing statistical methods (LMM) to examine the effects of error type, visual context, and interactions.
    * The finding that unimodal models can sometimes outperform multimodal models in this task is counter-intuitive and valuable.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The reliance on closed-source LLMs (Doubao and GPT-4o) is a limitation, as it hinders reproducibility and transparency. This makes it difficult to fully understand the underlying mechanisms driving the observed behaviors. Future work could explore similar experiments on open-source and locally deployable LLMs.
    * The study is limited to Chinese image descriptions. Generalizability to other languages needs further investigation.
    * While the study accounts for data contamination, it is still a concern, particularly given the widespread training of LLMs on internet data. The authors should also consider prompt contamination.
    * The selection of a relatively small number of images (9) is a potential limitation. A larger and more diverse set of images might provide more robust results.
    * While the LMM addresses normality issues, a deeper exploration of alternative statistical approaches or non-parametric tests might have strengthened the analysis.
    * The discussion could more deeply explore the ethical implications of using LLMs to evaluate NLG, particularly given the biases identified in this study.

* **Novelty and Significance:** The paper offers several novel insights. The systematic comparison of unimodal and multimodal conditions for LLM evaluation of NLG quality is novel.  The finding that LLMs struggle with colour errors compared to humans highlights a crucial gap in their perceptual grounding.  The counter-intuitive finding that DeepSeek-V3, a unimodal LLM, aligns well with human judgments (even outperforming some multimodal models) also points to interesting research avenues. It also helps to question current use of multimodal models over unimodal.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence how LLMs are used in NLG evaluation. The findings highlight the need for caution when using LLMs for subjective assessments, particularly in areas where human biases or perceptual grounding play a significant role. The results call for the development of methods to debias LLMs and improve their ability to accurately reflect human judgments. The demonstration of how multimodal LLMs do not influence humans may impact design decisions for LLM-based NLG pipelines.

**Justification for the Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7/10. While the paper is well-executed and addresses a relevant question, the limitations regarding closed-source models and the relatively small dataset hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution. The novelty of the findings is solid, and they will likely stimulate further research in this area, particularly in debiasing LLMs. The paper provides good insight for evaluating LLMs.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Knowledgeable-r1: Policy Optimization for Knowledge Exploration in Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05154v1)
- **Authors**: Chenyu Lin, Yilin Wen, Du Su, Fei Sun, Muhan Chen, Chenfu Bao, Zhonghou Lv
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a mainstream method for improving performance on knowledge-intensive tasks. However,current RAG systems often place too much emphasis on retrieved contexts. This can lead to reliance on inaccurate sources and overlook the model's inherent knowledge, especially when dealing with misleading or excessive information. To resolve this imbalance, we propose Knowledgeable-r1 that using joint sampling and define multi policy distributions in knowledge capability exploration to stimulate large language models'self-integrated utilization of parametric and contextual knowledge. Experiments show that Knowledgeable-r1 significantly enhances robustness and reasoning accuracy in both parameters and contextual conflict tasks and general RAG tasks, especially outperforming baselines by 17.07% in counterfactual scenarios and demonstrating consistent gains across RAG tasks. Our code are available at https://github.com/lcy80366872/ knowledgeable-r1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Knowledgeable-r1: Policy Optimization for Knowledge Exploration in Retrieval-Augmented Generation" addresses the issue of RAG systems over-relying on retrieved contexts, even when those contexts contain inaccurate or conflicting information. The authors propose Knowledgeable-r1, a reinforcement learning framework that encourages Large Language Models (LLMs) to better integrate their parametric knowledge with contextual information. The framework uses joint sampling and multi-policy distributions to stimulate the LLM's self-integrated utilization of both types of knowledge. The experiments demonstrate that Knowledgeable-r1 improves robustness and reasoning accuracy, particularly in scenarios with conflicting knowledge. The paper highlights improvements in counterfactual scenarios and consistent gains across general RAG tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem addressed is highly relevant. Current RAG systems often struggle with balancing retrieved and parametric knowledge, making them vulnerable to inaccurate contexts. This is a key challenge for improving the reliability of LLM applications.
    *   The proposed method, Knowledgeable-r1, is a novel approach that leverages reinforcement learning to tackle this knowledge integration problem. The idea of actively exploring and optimizing the use of both parametric and contextual knowledge is sound.
    *   The experimental results support the claims made. The paper shows significant improvements in ConflictQA and across a range of general RAG tasks. Furthermore, the ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components of Knowledgeable-r1.
    *   The analysis of upper limits and potential for improvements is valuable. It demonstrates that the method moves in a positive direction, closing the gap between RAG performance and theoretical correctness.
    *   The paper demonstrates model mergence can improve over the base models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the concept is novel, the technical details and implementation of Knowledgeable-r1 (the RL framework itself, including the reward function) could be described more clearly. The paper relies heavily on GRPO as a foundation, and it can be hard to disentangle the novelty that it is providing.
    *   The experimental setup is complex, and the number of baseline models and evaluation metrics is quite large.  While comprehensive, it somewhat obscures a clear understanding of where Knowledgeable-r1 excels. More focused comparisons would strengthen the arguments.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations related to abstention and situations where both knowledge sources are inaccurate. This is important, but it also points to areas where the method needs further development.
    *   The gains across general RAG tasks are often modest and not always statistically significant, and the performance is close to Search-R1. The improvements relative to already strong baselines on competitive benchmarks suggest that the problem may be close to being solved by simply using search.
    *   The study acknowledges the risk of misinformation but, lacks a thorough discussion of how Knowledgeable-r1 prevents the incorporation of fake knowledge.

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The idea of using a reinforcement learning framework with targeted exploration of both parametric and contextual knowledge in RAG is relatively novel. However, the reliance on GRPO as the base makes it incremental.
    *   The specific combination of joint sampling, multi-policy distributions, and advantage adjustment contributes some novelty.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper addresses a significant problem in RAG systems.
    *   The proposed method shows promising results.
    *   The RL-based approach could inspire further research into adaptive and knowledge-aware RAG systems.
    *   The insights gained from the ablation studies and performance analysis provide valuable guidance for future research.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**. Here's the rationale:

The paper addresses a clearly important and relevant problem in RAG.  The proposed method shows promising empirical results.  However, the technical exposition could be clearer and there are significant dependencies from GRPO. The evaluation is comprehensive, but the magnitude of the improvement is incremental. Overall, the work represents a useful contribution to the field.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Dissecting Bias in LLMs: A Mechanistic Interpretability Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05166v1)
- **Authors**: Bhavik Chandna, Zubair Bashir, Procheta Sen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are known to exhibit social, demographic, and gender biases, often as a consequence of the data on which they are trained. In this work, we adopt a mechanistic interpretability approach to analyze how such biases are structurally represented within models such as GPT-2 and Llama2. Focusing on demographic and gender biases, we explore different metrics to identify the internal edges responsible for biased behavior. We then assess the stability, localization, and generalizability of these components across dataset and linguistic variations. Through systematic ablations, we demonstrate that bias-related computations are highly localized, often concentrated in a small subset of layers. Moreover, the identified components change across fine-tuning settings, including those unrelated to bias. Finally, we show that removing these components not only reduces biased outputs but also affects other NLP tasks, such as named entity recognition and linguistic acceptability judgment because of the sharing of important components with these tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Dissecting Bias in LLMs: A Mechanistic Interpretability Perspective" investigates how social, demographic, and gender biases are structurally represented within Large Language Models (LLMs) such as GPT-2 and Llama-2.  It uses mechanistic interpretability techniques, primarily Edge Attribution Patching (EAP), to identify specific edges (connections between computational nodes) responsible for biased behavior.  The study analyzes the stability, localization, and generalizability of these identified components across datasets, linguistic variations, and fine-tuning settings.  The authors demonstrate that bias-related computations are often localized in specific layers and that the responsible components change with fine-tuning. They also show that removing these components, while reducing bias, can negatively affect performance on other NLP tasks, indicating shared functionality.  The paper proposes an inference-time debiasing strategy that leverages important edges.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic application of mechanistic interpretability techniques to dissect and understand bias in LLMs at the *edge* level, across *multiple* models (GPT-2 Small, GPT-2 Large, Llama-2) and *multiple* bias types (demographic and gender). While prior work has examined bias at the neuron or attention head level, this paper’s focus on edges offers a more granular and arguably more precise understanding of information flow contributing to bias. Examining the stability and generalizability across various factors such as grammatical structure, bias type, and fine-tuning regimes adds further value.
*   **Significance:** The study has significant implications for responsible AI development. By pinpointing specific edges responsible for bias, the work opens doors for targeted interventions that could mitigate harmful behaviors without wholesale retraining. The finding that bias-related components are localized offers hope for surgical debiasing. However, the identified trade-off between debiasing and performance on other NLP tasks is a crucial consideration. The proposed inference-time debiasing strategy, though promising, needs further validation.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:**  The paper offers a relatively thorough investigation, considering multiple LLMs, bias types, metrics, and evaluation scenarios.
    *   **Edge-level Focus:**  The emphasis on edges is a valuable contribution, going beyond neuron/head-centric views.
    *   **Mechanistic Interpretability:**  The use of mechanistic interpretability provides a more detailed and actionable understanding compared to purely statistical debiasing methods.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The inference-time debiasing strategy has the potential to reduce bias without extensive retraining.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Bias Types:**  The focus on demographic and gender bias, while important, limits the generalizability of findings to other types of bias (e.g., political, religious).
    *   **Edge Attribution Patching (EAP) Reliance:** The method relies heavily on the Edge Attribution Patching technique, which may have its own limitations or introduce approximations. Further validation with other MI techniques would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Inference-time Debiasing Validation:** The inference-time debiasing strategy is not rigorously tested on a wide range of datasets/benchmarks. The performance trade-offs need to be carefully quantified in various practical scenarios.
    *   **Limited Description of datasets:** Datasets for LLM training and fine-tuning are missing from the manuscript, which are necessary for reproducing results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in mechanistic interpretability for bias mitigation and responsible AI. It provides a roadmap for analyzing and intervening on LLMs to reduce bias. The identified trade-offs will encourage the development of more sophisticated debiasing strategies that minimize performance degradation. The findings on localization and stability will guide the design of more robust and trustworthy LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper represents a solid contribution to the field by systematically applying mechanistic interpretability to the problem of bias in LLMs. The focus on edges is novel and the exploration of stability and localization is valuable. However, the limited scope of bias types, reliance on a single MI technique, and need for more thorough validation of the debiasing strategy prevent it from achieving a higher score. The paper provides a good framework and promising preliminary results, but further research is needed to fully realize its potential.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ECoRAG: Evidentiality-guided Compression for Long Context RAG
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05167v1)
- **Authors**: Yeonseok Jeong, Jinsu Kim, Dohyeon Lee, Seung-won Hwang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable performance in Open-Domain Question Answering (ODQA) by leveraging external documents through Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). To reduce RAG overhead, from longer context, context compression is necessary. However, prior compression methods do not focus on filtering out non-evidential information, which limit the performance in LLM-based RAG. We thus propose Evidentiality-guided RAG, or \textbf{ECoRAG} framework. ECoRAG improves LLM performance by compressing retrieved documents based on evidentiality, ensuring whether answer generation is supported by the correct evidence. As an additional step, ECoRAG reflects whether the compressed content provides sufficient evidence, and if not, retrieves more until sufficient. Experiments show that ECoRAG improves LLM performance on ODQA tasks, outperforming existing compression methods. Furthermore, ECoRAG is highly cost-efficient, as it not only reduces latency but also minimizes token usage by retaining only the necessary information to generate the correct answer. Code is available at https://github.com/ldilab/ECoRAG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ECORAG: Evidentiality-guided Compression for Long Context RAG":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ECoRAG, an evidentiality-guided compression framework for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems dealing with long contexts. It addresses the problem of increased computational cost and potential performance degradation associated with long contexts in LLM-based RAG. ECoRAG compresses retrieved documents by prioritizing sentences based on their "evidentiality," which is defined by their ability to support the generation of the correct answer. This compression process involves: (1) sorting sentences based on evidentiality and (2) adaptively incorporating more information until the compressed content is deemed sufficiently evidential by an evaluator. The paper demonstrates that ECoRAG improves LLM performance on Open-Domain Question Answering (ODQA) tasks compared to existing compression methods, reduces latency, and minimizes token usage.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the evidentiality-guided compression approach. While context compression in RAG is not new, the paper's focus on filtering information based on its evidential support, specifically tuned for answer generation, is a valuable contribution.  The adaptive compression mechanism, where the system reflects on whether the compressed content is sufficient, adds another layer of refinement.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential to alleviate the computational burden and improve the accuracy of LLM-based RAG systems when dealing with long contexts. By efficiently filtering out irrelevant information, ECoRAG can enable more effective use of LLMs in scenarios where leveraging extensive external knowledge is crucial. The focus on reducing token usage translates directly to cost savings, a significant factor for real-world deployments. The ability to maintain or even improve accuracy compared to systems that use the entire raw context is also highly valuable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Definition of Evidentiality:** The paper provides a hierarchical definition of evidentiality that considers both the ability of a sentence to enable correct answer generation and the interference it might cause with other evidence.
    *   **Adaptive Compression:**  The adaptive reflection mechanism is a key strength.  It addresses the challenge of determining an appropriate compression ratio on a per-question basis.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on multiple ODQA datasets, comparing ECoRAG to various existing compression methods and demonstrating its superior performance in terms of accuracy, latency, and token usage.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies confirm the contribution of both the evidentiality-guided compressor and the adaptive compression evaluator.
    *   **Generalizability:** The investigation of generalizability across different LLMs and Retrievers is a positive sign, indicating the robustness of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost of Evidentiality Mining:** The paper acknowledges that mining evidentiality labels is computationally expensive, which could be a bottleneck in some scenarios. Although they try to mitigate this by reusing labels and employing an evaluator, the initial cost is still non-negligible. Further optimization could benefit the practical applicability.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs for Evidentiality Labeling:**  The reliance on LLMs to generate evidentiality labels introduces a potential dependence on the quality of the LLM and a risk of bias.  While the authors address this by training a smaller evaluator, it is still a potential limitation. Also, it may not be suitable for tasks where ground-truth answers aren't present for the Evidentiality Labeler.
    *   **Evaluation Metric Limitations:** Performance metrics in ODQA (EM/F1) may not fully capture the nuances of information filtering.
    *   **Lack of comparison in cost-sensitive settings:** Some methods mentioned in Section A.4 adapt rank and/or compression ratio to provide a performance under a token budget, which is something that this paper doesn't address.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of RAG systems for long contexts, particularly in applications where computational resources are limited or cost is a major concern.  It provides a valuable framework for reasoning about context compression based on evidentiality. Future research may build upon this work by exploring more efficient evidentiality mining techniques, alternative methods for adaptive compression, and extensions to other NLP tasks.

*   **Broader impact:** The framework helps to alleviate the computational burden of long-context problems, making LLMs accessible to a wider variety of scenarios.

*   **Overall Assessment:**  The paper presents a well-motivated and well-executed approach to context compression in RAG systems. The evidentiality-guided compression and adaptive reflection mechanisms are novel and effective. While there are some limitations related to the computational cost of evidentiality mining and reliance on LLMs for labeling, the strengths of the paper outweigh these weaknesses. It is a valuable contribution to the field of RAG and has the potential to influence future research and development in this area.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Associative Memory and Generative Diffusion in the Zero-noise Limit
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05178v1)
- **Authors**: Joshua Hess, Quaid Morris
- **Abstract**: Connections between generative diffusion and continuous-state associative memory models are studied. Morse-Smale dynamical systems are emphasized as universal approximators of gradient-based associative memory models and diffusion models as white-noise perturbed systems thereof. Universal properties of associative memory that follow from this description are described and used to characterize a generic transition from generation to memory as noise levels diminish. Structural stability inherited by Morse-Smale flows is shown to imply a notion of stability for diffusions at vanishing noise levels. Applied to one- and two-parameter families of gradients, this indicates stability at all but isolated points of associative memory learning landscapes and the learning and generation landscapes of diffusion models with gradient drift in the zero-noise limit, at which small sets of generic bifurcations characterize qualitative transitions between stable systems. Examples illustrating the characterization of these landscapes by sequences of these bifurcations are given, along with structural stability criterion for classic and modern Hopfield networks (equivalently, the attention mechanism).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Associative Memory and Generative Diffusion in the Zero-noise Limit":

**Summary:**

The paper explores the connections between continuous-state associative memory models and generative diffusion models. It emphasizes Morse-Smale dynamical systems as universal approximators for gradient-based associative memory models and their relationship to diffusion models as white-noise perturbed versions. It investigates universal properties of associative memory, characterizing the transition from generation to memory as noise levels decrease. The paper shows that structural stability inherited from Morse-Smale flows implies a notion of stability for diffusions at vanishing noise levels. It applies this to families of gradients, indicating stability except at isolated bifurcation points. Examples and structural stability criteria for Hopfield networks (and attention mechanisms) are provided. The paper aims to understand the global critical point structure and stability of continuous-state associative memory models using bifurcation theory and dynamical systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Perspective:**  The paper offers a novel perspective by connecting two seemingly disparate areas, associative memory and generative diffusion models, through the lens of dynamical systems and bifurcation theory.  This provides a unifying framework for understanding their behaviors.
    *   **Rigorous Mathematical Foundation:** The paper is rooted in rigorous mathematical concepts like Morse-Smale systems, structural stability, and large deviation theory, bringing a level of formality often lacking in purely empirical machine learning research.
    *   **Global Analysis:**  It moves beyond local analysis, typically focusing on fixed points, and offers a global characterization of the learning and generation landscapes using bifurcation theory.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The analysis of Hopfield networks and attention mechanisms, provides insight into the stability of these architectures.
    *   **Well-Structured and Comprehensive:** The paper is well-structured with a clear progression of ideas, definitions, and theorems. The inclusion of examples is helpful for illustrating the theoretical concepts.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Theoretical Focus:**  The paper is primarily theoretical. While connections to practical models are discussed, the empirical validation of the theoretical predictions is limited. Demonstrating the direct applicability of these analyses to improve the training or performance of state-of-the-art diffusion models would significantly strengthen its practical value.
    *   **Complexity:**  The reliance on advanced mathematical concepts might limit the accessibility of the paper to a broader machine learning audience.
    *   **Limited Empirical Results:** Many of the empirical results are simplified or illustrative rather than providing extensive experimental evaluations on current architectures.
    *   **Assumptions:**  The Morse-Smale assumption, while providing a strong theoretical foundation, might not always hold in practice for all real-world associative memory and diffusion models. While it's a generic condition, relaxing or addressing the implications of violating this assumption could be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Computational Complexity:** the paper does not offer an analysis of the computational efficiency of implementing results for the cases with high dimensionality.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The core novelty lies in bridging the gap between associative memory and diffusion models with a dynamical systems framework using Morse-Smale theory. The global analysis using bifurcation theory is also a significant contribution. The paper has potential impact by:

    *   **Providing a theoretical basis for understanding and improving the stability and robustness of associative memory and generative diffusion models.**
    *   **Guiding the design of new architectures with improved learning dynamics.**
    *   **Opening up new avenues for research combining dynamical systems theory and machine learning.**

However, the lack of extensive empirical results and the complexity of the mathematics slightly reduce the immediate impact.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid theoretical contributions and novel perspective, tempered by the limitations in empirical validation and accessibility. The paper's mathematical rigor and attempt at global analysis are commendable. However, the lack of direct, demonstrable improvements to practical model training or performance holds it back from being a truly exceptional contribution. If the theoretical insights could be more clearly translated into actionable guidelines for practitioners and accompanied by compelling empirical evidence, the impact would be significantly higher.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### On the Comprehensibility of Multi-structured Financial Documents using LLMs and Pre-processing Tools
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05182v1)
- **Authors**: Shivani Upadhyay, Messiah Ataey, Shariyar Murtuza, Yifan Nie, Jimmy Lin
- **Abstract**: The proliferation of complex structured data in hybrid sources, such as PDF documents and web pages, presents unique challenges for current Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in providing accurate answers. Despite the recent advancements of MLLMs, they still often falter when interpreting intricately structured information, such as nested tables and multi-dimensional plots, leading to hallucinations and erroneous outputs. This paper explores the capabilities of LLMs and MLLMs in understanding and answering questions from complex data structures found in PDF documents by leveraging industrial and open-source tools as part of a pre-processing pipeline. Our findings indicate that GPT-4o, a popular MLLM, achieves an accuracy of 56% on multi-structured documents when fed documents directly, and that integrating pre-processing tools raises the accuracy of LLMs to 61.3% for GPT-4o and 76% for GPT-4, and with lower overall cost. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/OGCDS/FinancialQA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for question answering (QA) over complex, multi-structured financial documents (PDFs). It explores a pipeline that incorporates industrial and open-source pre-processing tools (Azure Document Intelligence, pypdf, ChartVLM) to extract text, tables, and charts from these documents before feeding them to the LLMs. The study compares the performance of GPT-4o, with and without pre-processing, as well as GPT-3.5 Turbo and GPT-4 on text-only data. The key findings are that: 1) Directly using GPT-4o on raw documents achieves only 56% accuracy; 2) Using pre-processing tools significantly improves accuracy, up to 76% with GPT-4 (text-only); and 3)  The pre-processing pipeline is also more cost-effective compared to commercial solutions. The paper analyzes limitations in chart and table interpretation, contributing a dataset of 75 question-answer pairs based on financial documents.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** The problem addressed is highly relevant in industries like finance and insurance, where analysts need to extract information from complex documents.
    *   **Rigorous Experimentation:** The paper conducts well-defined experiments comparing different LLMs and pre-processing strategies on a concrete task (financial QA).
    *   **Cost-Effectiveness Analysis:** The cost comparison is valuable, demonstrating the economic benefits of the proposed pre-processing pipeline.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The paper identifies and discusses specific challenges related to chart and table interpretation, providing valuable insights for future research.
    *   **Useful Dataset:** While private, the authors’ efforts to create a test set of realistic financial question-answer pairs addresses a gap in existing benchmarks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** The concept of using pre-processing to improve LLM performance isn't entirely new. RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) is a well-established technique. The novelty lies primarily in the specific combination of tools and the focus on a particular domain (financial documents).
    *   **Lack of a Strong Baseline:** The paper compares against a limited set of commercial offerings (LlamaParse, Vertex AI, Claude 3). More thorough benchmarking against existing RAG systems and document understanding tools (e.g., specialized PDF extraction libraries) would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Private Dataset:** The dataset is not publicly available, making it difficult for other researchers to replicate or extend the work.
    *   **Limited Fine-tuning Analysis:** The attempt to fine-tune ChartVLM is glossed over, with minimal detail about the specific strategies and data used. Deeper analysis would be more insightful.
    *   **Generalizability:**  The results are based on a specific set of financial documents and a fixed set of tools. The generalizability of the findings to other types of documents or different pre-processing frameworks is uncertain.

*   **Significance and Impact:**

    *   The paper provides practical guidance on building LLM-based QA systems for financial documents, with a focus on cost-effectiveness and improved accuracy.
    *   The identified challenges in chart and table interpretation can guide future research directions in multi-modal LLMs.
    *   The results can influence businesses in the financial sector for adopting MLLM-based Q&A solutions

*   **Novelty:** The most novel aspect is the domain-specific application of pre-processing combined with particular LLMs. The combination of Azure Document Intelligence, ChartVLM, and a specific RAG architecture has not been extensively explored in the financial document domain to the authors’ knowledge. The analysis of cost is also a significant strength.

**Overall Score:**

Score: 6.5

**Justification:**

The paper addresses a relevant practical problem, provides useful insights into LLM and MLLM performance on complex documents, and demonstrates the benefits of pre-processing. It also includes a cost analysis which makes it valuable for businesses considering adopting this technology. It has practical significance, but the methodological innovation is somewhat limited. The private dataset and limited exploration of fine-tuning and other pre-processing strategies lower the overall score. A thorough comparison with other similar systems, more in-depth analysis of different fine-tuning or pre-processing configurations, and a publicly available benchmark would improve the paper significantly. Although it is well written and thoroughly demonstrates its points, the relative incremental value in terms of a scholarly contribution of this paper could be improved.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### TreeRPO: Tree Relative Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05183v1)
- **Authors**: Zhicheng Yang, Zhijiang Guo, Yinya Huang, Xiaodan Liang, Yiwei Wang, Jing Tang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable reasoning capabilities through Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) methods. However, a key limitation of existing approaches is that rewards defined at the full trajectory level provide insufficient guidance for optimizing the intermediate steps of a reasoning process. To address this, we introduce \textbf{\name}, a novel method that estimates the mathematical expectations of rewards at various reasoning steps using tree sampling. Unlike prior methods that rely on a separate step reward model, \name directly estimates these rewards through this sampling process. Building on the group-relative reward training mechanism of GRPO, \name innovatively computes rewards based on step-level groups generated during tree sampling. This advancement allows \name to produce fine-grained and dense reward signals, significantly enhancing the learning process and overall performance of LLMs. Experimental results demonstrate that our \name algorithm substantially improves the average Pass@1 accuracy of Qwen-2.5-Math on test benchmarks, increasing it from 19.0\% to 35.5\%. Furthermore, \name significantly outperforms GRPO by 2.9\% in performance while simultaneously reducing the average response length by 18.1\%, showcasing its effectiveness and efficiency. Our code will be available at \href{https://github.com/yangzhch6/TreeRPO}{https://github.com/yangzhch6/TreeRPO}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the TREERPO paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TREERPO, a novel reinforcement learning method designed to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  TREERPO addresses the limitations of trajectory-level reward signals by estimating rewards at various reasoning steps through a tree-based sampling approach.  It builds upon the Group-Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) framework, innovating by computing rewards based on step-level groups generated during tree sampling. This provides fine-grained reward signals without relying on separate step reward models.  The authors demonstrate that TREERPO significantly improves the accuracy of Qwen-2.5-Math on mathematical benchmarks compared to GRPO, while also reducing response length.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The core novelty of TREERPO lies in its tree-based sampling approach to approximate step-level rewards in a reward-model-free setting. This is a significant improvement over existing methods that rely on trajectory-level rewards or require a separate reward model. The combination of tree-based sampling with GRPO's group-relative reward training mechanism is also a novel contribution.
    *   While GRPO's general framework exists, the adaptation and extension to a tree-based sampling strategy for deriving step-level rewards is a non-trivial contribution. The authors clearly define how the tree structure is built, and how rewards are propagated and aggregated.
    *   The advantage computation method adapted for continuous reward is a contribution, although maybe minor.

*   **Significance:**

    *   Improving the reasoning abilities of LLMs is a crucial area of research, and TREERPO presents a potentially valuable technique for this. The experimental results, showing substantial gains in accuracy on challenging mathematical benchmarks, demonstrate the practical significance of the approach.
    *   The reduction in response length while maintaining or improving accuracy is also significant, as it indicates more efficient and precise reasoning.
    *   The method offers an efficient and scalable way to achieve dense reward signals without needing a separate reward model. This addresses a crucial bottleneck in scaling reinforcement learning for LLMs.
    *   The experimental methodology is sound, and the paper provides sufficient details for reproducibility.
    *   The code availability enhances the paper's significance and facilitates further research in this area.
    *   The results focus only on mathematical reasoning. It would be beneficial to test on different types of reasoning tasks (e.g. commonsense reasoning).
    *   While promising, the paper only uses Qwen-2.5-Math, a specific model. Evaluation on other models is crucial for generalization.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-written.
    *   Addresses a critical limitation of trajectory-level rewards.
    *   Introduces a novel tree-based sampling technique.
    *   Demonstrates significant performance improvements on mathematical benchmarks.
    *   Reduces response length, indicating more efficient reasoning.
    *   Provides details for reproducibility and offers code availability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation is primarily focused on mathematical reasoning tasks. Broader applicability to other types of reasoning should be explored.
    *   Experiments are limited to Qwen-2.5-Math, further validations is desired.
    *   The engineering efficiency optimization is mentioned as future work. Without an optimized sampling procedure, the method might be impractically slow.
    *   The precise step segmentation based on token length might be too basic and require improvement.

**Overall:**

TREERPO presents a novel and potentially impactful approach to improve the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. The combination of tree-based sampling and group-relative reward computation is a significant contribution. The paper provides strong empirical evidence demonstrating the effectiveness of the method on challenging mathematical benchmarks. While the evaluation could be broader, and certain aspects could be optimized, TREERPO offers a promising direction for future research in this area.

**Score: 8**

*Rationale:*  TREERPO offers a valuable advancement in reinforcement learning for LLMs. The novelty of combining tree-based sampling with GRPO's reward mechanism is significant and is backed by strong experimental results on math benchmarks. While the reliance on Qwen-2.5-Math and lack of broader reasoning evaluations slightly lowers the score, the clarity of the paper, the potential impact on the field, and code availability justify the assigned score. Without code or more general experiments, I might have scored it 7.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Counterfactual reasoning: an analysis of in-context emergence
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05188v1)
- **Authors**: Moritz Miller, Bernhard Schölkopf, Siyuan Guo
- **Abstract**: Large-scale neural language models (LMs) exhibit remarkable performance in in-context learning: the ability to learn and reason the input context on the fly without parameter update. This work studies in-context counterfactual reasoning in language models, that is, to predict the consequences of changes under hypothetical scenarios. We focus on studying a well-defined synthetic setup: a linear regression task that requires noise abduction, where accurate prediction is based on inferring and copying the contextual noise from factual observations. We show that language models are capable of counterfactual reasoning in this controlled setup and provide insights that counterfactual reasoning for a broad class of functions can be reduced to a transformation on in-context observations; we find self-attention, model depth, and data diversity in pre-training drive performance in Transformers. More interestingly, our findings extend beyond regression tasks and show that Transformers can perform noise abduction on sequential data, providing preliminary evidence on the potential for counterfactual story generation. Our code is available under https://github.com/moXmiller/counterfactual-reasoning.git .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of large language models (LLMs) to perform in-context counterfactual reasoning. It focuses on a controlled synthetic setup involving a linear regression task where the model must infer and copy contextual noise from factual observations to predict outcomes under hypothetical scenarios. The authors demonstrate that LLMs are capable of this type of counterfactual reasoning and find that performance is influenced by factors such as self-attention, model depth, and data diversity during pre-training. The findings are extended to sequential data, offering preliminary evidence for the potential of counterfactual story generation.  The paper connects counterfactual reasoning to transformations on in-context observations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper makes a novel contribution by examining in-context counterfactual reasoning in LLMs within a *well-defined* and *controlled* synthetic setting. Prior work has explored counterfactuals in language models, but this work offers a specific, mechanistic analysis of how they perform this task, particularly focusing on noise abduction. Connecting counterfactual reasoning with transformation of in-context examples provides a theoretical perspective.

*   **Significance:** The study's significance lies in its attempt to understand the inner workings of LLMs' reasoning abilities. By demonstrating that LLMs can perform counterfactual reasoning, even in simplified settings, the paper highlights their potential for more complex reasoning tasks. The extension to sequential data hints at broader applications, such as improved story generation and causal inference. If counterfactual reasoning capabilities can be reliably demonstrated and controlled, this can be highly relevant for improving AI safety and scientific discovery.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Controlled Setting:** The use of a synthetic environment allows for precise control over the causal structure and noise, enabling a rigorous evaluation of the model's performance.
    *   **Clear Research Question:**  The paper clearly defines its research question and provides concrete metrics for evaluating the model's performance.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The authors conduct a comprehensive analysis of the factors that influence counterfactual reasoning, including model architecture, pre-training data, and attention mechanisms.
    *   **Theoretical grounding:** The paper grounds its study in the counterfactual framework of Pearl [2009] and connects it to exchangeability and De Finetti's theorem.
    *   **Connection to transformations**: Lemma 1 provides a theoretical insight into how counterfactual reasoning can be framed as a transformation over in-context examples.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Data:** While the controlled setting is a strength, it also limits the generalizability of the findings.  The real world is significantly more complex than the linear regression task used in the paper.  While some extension to more complex functions is offered, they remain in a mathematically defined space.
    *   **Simplified Task:** The counterfactual reasoning task itself is relatively simple. It primarily focuses on noise abduction and copying, which may not capture the full complexity of counterfactual reasoning in natural language.
    *   **Limited Scale:** The paper primarily works with relatively small GPT-2 models. Larger models and datasets are necessary to confirm the findings and explore the potential of counterfactual reasoning in more complex scenarios.
    *   **Early Stage for Story Generation:** The preliminary evidence for counterfactual story generation is promising but requires further investigation.  The connection to real-world narrative is not strong.
    *   **Oversimplified Natural Language Connection**: The natural language connection described in the appendix is very basic and almost "toy" setting, failing to address the complexities of ambiguity in real language.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can stimulate further research into the reasoning abilities of LLMs and the role of pre-training data and model architecture in shaping these abilities. It can also inform the development of methods for improving the safety and reliability of LLMs.

**Overall:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a rigorous analysis of in-context counterfactual reasoning in LLMs within a controlled synthetic setting. While the findings may not directly translate to real-world scenarios, they offer valuable insights into the mechanisms underlying LLMs' reasoning abilities and provide a foundation for future research. The study is well-designed and well-executed. The major limitation is the artificial setup, which may not generalize directly to more complex and nuanced situations.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Quantifying Cross-Modality Memorization in Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05198v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxin Wen, Yangsibo Huang, Tom Goldstein, Ravi Kumar, Badih Ghazi, Chiyuan Zhang
- **Abstract**: Understanding what and how neural networks memorize during training is crucial, both from the perspective of unintentional memorization of potentially sensitive information and from the standpoint of effective knowledge acquisition for real-world, knowledge-intensive tasks. While previous studies primarily investigate memorization within a single modality, such as text memorization in large language models or image memorization in diffusion models, unified multimodal models are becoming increasingly prevalent in practical applications. In this work, we focus on the unique characteristics of cross-modality memorization and conduct a systematic study centered on vision-language models. To facilitate controlled experiments, we first introduce a synthetic persona dataset comprising diverse synthetic person images and textual descriptions. We quantify factual knowledge memorization and cross-modal transferability by training models on a single modality and evaluating their performance in the other. Our results reveal that facts learned in one modality transfer to the other, but a significant gap exists between recalling information in the source and target modalities. Furthermore, we observe that this gap exists across various scenarios, including more capable models, machine unlearning, and the multi-hop case. At the end, we propose a baseline method to mitigate this challenge. We hope our study can inspire future research on developing more robust multimodal learning techniques to enhance cross-modal transferability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Quantifying Cross-Modality Memorization in Vision-Language Models" investigates how vision-language models (VLMs) memorize factual knowledge across image and text modalities. The authors create a synthetic persona dataset to conduct controlled experiments, training VLMs to associate names with images and textual descriptions. The study quantifies factual knowledge memorization and cross-modal transferability by training models on one modality and evaluating their performance on the other. Key findings include: factual knowledge learned in one modality transfers to the other, but a performance gap exists between recalling information in the "source" and "target" modalities. The study also explores challenges like a cross-modal "multi-hop curse" and the impact of machine unlearning on different modalities. Finally, the paper explores mitigation strategies such as data augmentation. The work also highlights that VLMs can unintentionally memorize irrelevant visual PII, thus calling for stronger data filtering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's exploration of *cross-modal* memorization in VLMs is a valuable contribution. While memorization in unimodal models (text or image alone) has been extensively studied, the interaction and transfer of memorized knowledge between modalities is relatively less explored. The synthetic dataset is a useful tool for controlled experimentation. The work on unintentional PII memorization is interesting and alarming.

*   **Significance:** The findings have implications for building more robust and trustworthy multimodal systems. Understanding cross-modal transfer is crucial for privacy (preventing leakage of sensitive information) and performance optimization (leveraging knowledge across modalities). The insights into cross-modal unlearning and the multi-hop curse are also valuable. The paper's focus on factual knowledge memorization is realistic and aligned with practical applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The use of a synthetic dataset allows for rigorous control over the training data and the types of facts memorized.
    *   The experimental design is well-structured, systematically investigating different training scenarios, mitigation strategies, and model sizes.
    *   The findings are clearly presented and supported by experimental evidence.
    *   The paper identifies key challenges and gaps in cross-modal memorization, providing directions for future research.
    *   The exploration of unintentional memorization of PII is an important contribution highlighting privacy concerns.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The synthetic nature of the data, while allowing for control, may limit the generalizability of the findings to real-world datasets.  It would be beneficial to see experiments on real-world data to confirm the observations.
    *   The mitigation strategies explored are relatively simple. More sophisticated approaches might be needed to address the challenges of cross-modal transfer.
    *   While the paper introduces the problem of unintentional PII memorization, the analysis of this phenomenon is preliminary. The impact and severity of this threat could be further explored with different PII types and more complex tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research in cross-modal memorization, unlearning, and privacy risks.  The insights into the limitations of current VLMs and the challenges of cross-modal transfer can inform the development of better training techniques and model architectures.  The work on unintentional PII memorization also raises awareness of potential privacy issues in multimodal AI systems.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7.

*   It is *not* a 9 or 10 because it relies heavily on a synthetic dataset, limiting the immediate real-world impact.  Also, the mitigation strategies explored are somewhat basic.
*   However, the paper is substantially *more* than a 5 or 6 because it makes a significant contribution by systematically investigating a largely unexplored area (cross-modal memorization in VLMs).  The methodology is rigorous, and the findings are well-supported. The study successfully identifies key challenges (the performance gap, multi-hop curse, unlearning difficulties) and highlights an important privacy issue related to PII.  The work points to promising future research directions.
