### The Common Pile v0.1: An 8TB Dataset of Public Domain and Openly Licensed Text
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05209v1)
- **Authors**: Nikhil Kandpal, Brian Lester, Colin Raffel, Sebastian Majstorovic, Stella Biderman, Baber Abbasi, Luca Soldaini, Enrico Shippole, A. Feder Cooper, Aviya Skowron, John Kirchenbauer, Shayne Longpre, Lintang Sutawika, Alon Albalak, Zhenlin Xu, Guilherme Penedo, Loubna Ben Allal, Elie Bakouch, John David Pressman, Honglu Fan, Dashiell Stander, Guangyu Song, Aaron Gokaslan, Tom Goldstein, Brian R. Bartoldson, Bhavya Kailkhura, Tyler Murray
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are typically trained on enormous quantities of unlicensed text, a practice that has led to scrutiny due to possible intellectual property infringement and ethical concerns. Training LLMs on openly licensed text presents a first step towards addressing these issues, but prior data collection efforts have yielded datasets too small or low-quality to produce performant LLMs. To address this gap, we collect, curate, and release the Common Pile v0.1, an eight terabyte collection of openly licensed text designed for LLM pretraining. The Common Pile comprises content from 30 sources that span diverse domains including research papers, code, books, encyclopedias, educational materials, audio transcripts, and more. Crucially, we validate our efforts by training two 7 billion parameter LLMs on text from the Common Pile: Comma v0.1-1T and Comma v0.1-2T, trained on 1 and 2 trillion tokens respectively. Both models attain competitive performance to LLMs trained on unlicensed text with similar computational budgets, such as Llama 1 and 2 7B. In addition to releasing the Common Pile v0.1 itself, we also release the code used in its creation as well as the training mixture and checkpoints for the Comma v0.1 models.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided document. Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary**

The paper introduces the Common Pile v0.1, an 8TB dataset of public domain and openly licensed text intended for training large language models (LLMs). It addresses concerns surrounding intellectual property infringement and ethical considerations associated with training LLMs on unlicensed text. The Common Pile contains data from 30 diverse sources, including research papers, code, books, government documents, and audio transcripts. The authors trained two 7-billion parameter LLMs, Comma v0.1-1T and Comma v0.1-2T, on this dataset, achieving performance comparable to LLMs trained on unlicensed text (Llama 1 and 2 7B). The paper also details the data collection, cleaning, filtering, and mixing procedures used to create the dataset. They release the Common Pile v0.1, the trained Comma v0.1 models, and associated code.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the **scale and curation of openly licensed data specifically tailored for LLM pre-training**. While previous efforts created datasets of this type (OLC, Common Corpus, KL3M), they were smaller, less diverse, or had other limitations. The authors explicitly focused on rigorous license due diligence, avoiding issues like "license laundering."  The training of LLMs to the scale of Llama 1/2 on this fully openly licensed data is a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its contribution to the ethical and legal discourse surrounding LLM training. By demonstrating that competitive performance can be achieved using only openly licensed data, the authors provide a pathway for developing LLMs with reduced legal and ethical risks.  It offers a potential first step in bridging the growing divide between LLM developers and content creators. The release of the dataset, models, and code promotes reproducibility and further research in this area.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Data Collection and Curation:** The paper provides thorough details on the data sources, license validation, and pre-processing steps.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors perform controlled experiments comparing models trained on the Common Pile to those trained on other datasets, including both openly licensed and unlicensed data. The ablation studies on inclusion of data from the Data Provenance Initative is well justified.
    *   **Open Release:** The release of the dataset, models, and code promotes reproducibility and further research.
    *   **Addresses a key Ethical Concern**: Training LLMs on only openly licensed text to mitigate legal risks.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Scale:** While the results are encouraging, demonstrating similar performance to Llama 1 and 2 7B, these are not state-of-the-art models. It remains to be seen if this approach can scale to the largest LLMs, which can be challenging given the limited data supply. Larger models will likely require significantly more data, a potential bottleneck. It would've also been interesting to observe the performance of the model after 3T or 4T tokens training to more thoroughly observe saturation effects.
    *   **Heuristic Data Mixing:** While data mixing is essential, it is concerning that heuristics were used to up/down-weight sources rather than relying on an automated approach like MixMin [178].
    *   **Over-Repetitive data mixture:** The training procedure for Comma v0.1-2T involves simply repeating the 1T mixture a second time and this comes at the cost of excessive data repetition.
    *   **English Centric:** Focused primarily on English language, which limits its global applicability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the development of future LLMs, particularly in research settings where ethical and legal compliance are prioritized. It could also spur further efforts to collect and curate openly licensed data, addressing the data scarcity issue.
* Given the limitations in model scale, data mixing, and over-repetitive data mixtures, the contributions in creating an LLM solely on openly licensed and public domain data is a significant and timely contribution to the literature.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by creating and releasing a large, curated dataset of openly licensed text and demonstrating its viability for training competitive LLMs. The rigorous data collection and evaluation methodology strengthens the findings. While the model scale is limited, the results suggest a promising path forward for ethical and legally compliant LLM development. The lack of automated data mixing and limitations related to data mixture repetition and English centric data somewhat temper the impact, but the paper's contribution to the discourse around responsible AI development are substantial.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM-First Search: Self-Guided Exploration of the Solution Space
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05213v1)
- **Authors**: Nathan Herr, Tim Rocktäschel, Roberta Raileanu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable improvements in reasoning and planning through increased test-time compute, often by framing problem-solving as a search process. While methods like Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) have proven effective in some domains, their reliance on fixed exploration hyperparameters limits their adaptability across tasks of varying difficulty, rendering them impractical or expensive in certain settings. In this paper, we propose \textbf{LLM-First Search (LFS)}, a novel \textit{LLM Self-Guided Search} method that removes the need for pre-defined search strategies by empowering the LLM to autonomously control the search process via self-guided exploration. Rather than relying on external heuristics or hardcoded policies, the LLM evaluates whether to pursue the current search path or explore alternative branches based on its internal scoring mechanisms. This enables more flexible and context-sensitive reasoning without requiring manual tuning or task-specific adaptation. We evaluate LFS on Countdown and Sudoku against three classic widely-used search algorithms, Tree-of-Thoughts' Breadth First Search (ToT-BFS), Best First Search (BestFS), and MCTS, each of which have been used to achieve SotA results on a range of challenging reasoning tasks. We found that LFS (1) performs better on more challenging tasks without additional tuning, (2) is more computationally efficient compared to the other methods, especially when powered by a stronger model, (3) scales better with stronger models, due to its LLM-First design, and (4) scales better with increased compute budget. Our code is publicly available at \href{https://github.com/NathanHerr/LLM-First-Search}{LLM-First-Search}.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLM-First Search (LFS), a novel approach to search algorithms for Large Language Models (LLMs). Unlike traditional methods like Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS), which rely on predefined search strategies and hyperparameters, LFS empowers the LLM to autonomously control the search process through self-guided exploration.  The LLM evaluates the promise of the current search path and decides whether to continue or explore alternatives, based on its internal scoring mechanisms. LFS is evaluated on Countdown and Sudoku against traditional search algorithms like Tree-of-Thoughts (ToT), Best-First Search (BestFS), and MCTS. The results indicate that LFS performs better on more challenging tasks without additional tuning, is computationally more efficient, scales better with stronger models and increased compute budget.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of LFS, allowing the LLM to drive the search strategy itself, is a significant departure from traditional search algorithms adapted for LLMs.  While LLMs have been used as components *within* search algorithms (e.g., for value estimation), giving the LLM complete control over the exploration-exploitation trade-off is novel. The design fundamentally rethinks the role of search, from an external process guiding the LLM to an internalized, language-driven mechanism. The prompts detailed in the Appendix are critical in enabling this.

* **Significance:**  The potential impact of LFS is considerable.  The traditional reliance on carefully tuned hyperparameters in methods like MCTS is a significant bottleneck. By automating the exploration strategy, LFS addresses a key limitation, making LLM-based reasoning more adaptable and practical. The improved computational efficiency and scalability are also highly valuable, particularly as LLMs grow larger and more capable. Demonstrated success on two benchmark tasks (Countdown and Sudoku) adds credibility to LFS and opens exciting avenues for future research.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Adaptive Search Strategy:** The core strength of LFS is its adaptability, eliminating the need for manual tuning of exploration parameters, making the algorithm more robust across different tasks and model capabilities.
    * **Computational Efficiency and Scalability:** Empirical results clearly demonstrate LFS's improved computational efficiency and scalability with stronger models and increased compute budget. This is crucial for practical applications of LLMs in complex reasoning tasks.
    * **Clear Evaluation:**  The paper presents a well-defined problem setting, clear evaluation metrics, and thorough comparisons against strong baselines. The inclusion of performance profiles and AUP scores strengthens the robustness of the evaluation.
    * **Open Source Code:** Providing the code promotes reproducibility and allows for future work to build upon LFS.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Task Diversity:**  While Countdown and Sudoku are valuable benchmarks, they are relatively structured and deterministic.  It remains to be seen how well LFS generalizes to more complex, real-world tasks with higher degrees of uncertainty or requiring interaction with external environments.
    * **Reliance on Prompt Engineering:** The performance of LFS heavily depends on the design of the exploration and evaluation prompts. Although the prompts are detailed in the Appendix, their sensitivity and generalizability require further investigation.  Different prompts may yield varying performance, and there isn't a clear methodology presented for optimizing these prompts.
    * **Limited Exploration of Incremental Improvements:** The paper focuses on the core innovation of LFS but doesn't extensively explore potential incremental improvements such as the integration of self-consistency, reflection or debate - components that could enhance LFS further.
    * **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:**  While the empirical results are compelling, the paper lacks a theoretical analysis of LFS's convergence properties or exploration efficiency.
    * **Computational Budget Restrictions:** The limited scope of test runs prevents the full realization of LFS’s potential, especially in high-complexity situations.

* **Potential Influence:** LFS has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM-based reasoning and problem-solving. By reimagining search as an integrated, language-driven mechanism, LFS offers a promising direction for developing more adaptable, efficient, and scalable LLM agents. Its impact would be further amplified when combined with other enhancements.

**Score:** 8/10

**Justification:** LFS presents a novel and significant advancement in search algorithms for LLMs, addressing a key limitation of existing methods through its adaptive self-guided exploration.  The empirical results support its improved efficiency and scalability.  However, the limited task diversity, dependence on prompt engineering, lack of a theoretical analysis, and computational budget restrictions prevent a higher score.  The paper establishes a strong foundation, and future work addressing these limitations has the potential to greatly enhance its impact.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Improving Low-Resource Morphological Inflection via Self-Supervised Objectives
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05227v1)
- **Authors**: Adam Wiemerslage, Katharina von der Wense
- **Abstract**: Self-supervised objectives have driven major advances in NLP by leveraging large-scale unlabeled data, but such resources are scarce for many of the world's languages. Surprisingly, they have not been explored much for character-level tasks, where smaller amounts of data have the potential to be beneficial. We investigate the effectiveness of self-supervised auxiliary tasks for morphological inflection -- a character-level task highly relevant for language documentation -- in extremely low-resource settings, training encoder-decoder transformers for 19 languages and 13 auxiliary objectives. Autoencoding yields the best performance when unlabeled data is very limited, while character masked language modeling (CMLM) becomes more effective as data availability increases. Though objectives with stronger inductive biases influence model predictions intuitively, they rarely outperform standard CMLM. However, sampling masks based on known morpheme boundaries consistently improves performance, highlighting a promising direction for low-resource morphological modeling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of self-supervised objectives to improve low-resource morphological inflection. Recognizing the scarcity of large-scale unlabeled data for many languages, the authors focus on character-level tasks. They experiment with various self-supervised auxiliary tasks, derived from masked language modeling (MLM), using an encoder-decoder transformer architecture across 19 languages. The study investigates how different masking strategies (uniform, suffix-biased, prefix-biased, and segment-based) and objectives (CMLM vs. T5-style span masking) impact performance in a multitask learning setting, where the primary task is morphological inflection. The results suggest that autoencoding performs best with very limited unlabeled data, while MLM becomes more effective as data availability increases. The study also finds evidence that while inductive biases can influence model predictions, uniform masking often provides the best overall performance, except when masks are sampled based on known morpheme boundaries.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper provides a relatively thorough exploration of self-supervised auxiliary tasks for a character-level task in extremely low-resource settings. While using self-supervised learning for NLP is not novel, the systematic comparison of various masking strategies *specifically at the character level* *and focusing on morphological inflection in low-resource scenarios* demonstrates a useful contribution. The segment masking based on oracle morpheme boundaries is a particularly intriguing idea, pointing to a potential path forward. However, many of the individual components (MLM, transformer architecture, auxiliary tasks) are well-established. The novelty primarily resides in the *combination* of these elements and their application to a specific low-resource problem.

*   **Significance:** Morphological inflection is a vital task for many languages, particularly for those with limited resources and for language documentation. The paper's exploration of techniques that can improve performance in these scenarios is highly relevant. The findings, even with their nuances, offer practical insights for practitioners working on low-resource language processing. For example, the recommendation to use autoencoding when unlabeled data is very limited is useful. Additionally, their exploration indicates that using morpheme boundaries for masking may be a promising direction for improving self-supervised objectives for morphological tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Experimentation:** The paper examines a wide range of self-supervised objectives and masking strategies, providing a detailed empirical analysis.
    *   **Focus on Low-Resource Scenarios:** The study directly addresses the challenges of limited data, which is a significant constraint in many real-world language processing applications.
    *   **Typological Diversity:** Experiments are conducted across 19 languages, adding to the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Clear Presentation of Results:** The results are presented with clarity and precision, using tables and figures to effectively communicate the findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Individual Components:** The individual self-supervised objectives and masking strategies are not entirely new. The main contribution is their combination and evaluation in a specific context.
    *   **Dependence on Oracle Morpheme Boundaries:** The segment masking approach relies on access to gold-standard morpheme boundaries, which may not be readily available in all low-resource settings. This reduces the practicality of this method. Further research is needed to determine how to approximate these boundaries without human annotation.
    *   **Somewhat Inconclusive Results:** While the paper presents a comprehensive analysis, the overall findings are not entirely conclusive. Uniform masking often performs best, suggesting that more sophisticated inductive biases are not always beneficial, particularly for character-level tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's insights could inform future research on self-supervised learning for low-resource language processing, particularly for tasks related to morphology and character-level modeling. The findings could also be valuable for practitioners working on language documentation and indigenous language education. The direction of using morpheme boundaries for masking is an insightful direction for future research.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the above assessment, the paper is assigned a score of 6. The strengths of comprehensive experimentation, clear presentation, and typological diversity contribute positively. However, limited novelty in individual components, the reliance on oracle morpheme boundaries, and somewhat inconclusive results justify the mid-range score. The paper provides a solid, practical investigation of relevant techniques with some promising directions, but doesn't introduce groundbreaking new methods or produce transformative findings. It's a useful contribution to a niche area, but lacks broad, high-impact novelty.

**Score: 6**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Diagonal Batching Unlocks Parallelism in Recurrent Memory Transformers for Long Contexts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05229v1)
- **Authors**: Danil Sivtsov, Ivan Rodkin, Gleb Kuzmin, Yuri Kuratov, Ivan Oseledets
- **Abstract**: Transformer models struggle with long-context inference due to their quadratic time and linear memory complexity. Recurrent Memory Transformers (RMTs) offer a solution by reducing the asymptotic cost to linear time and constant memory usage. However, their memory update mechanism leads to sequential execution, causing a performance bottleneck. We introduce Diagonal Batching, a scheduling scheme that unlocks parallelism across segments in RMTs while preserving exact recurrence. This approach eliminates the sequential constraint, enabling efficient GPU inference even for single long-context inputs without complex batching and pipelining techniques. Because the technique is purely a run-time computation reordering, existing RMT models adopt it with no retraining. Applied to a LLaMA-1B ARMT model, Diagonal Batching yields a 3.3x speedup over standard full-attention LLaMA-1B and a 1.8x speedup over the sequential RMT implementation on 131,072-token sequences. By removing sequential bottleneck, Diagonal Batching reduces inference cost and latency, thereby strengthening RMTs as a practical solution for real-world, long-context applications.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper. Here's a summary, a critical evaluation, and a score:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diagonal Batching, a scheduling scheme designed to improve the inference speed of Recurrent Memory Transformers (RMTs) and Parallel Recurrent Memory Transformers (PRMTs) for long-context tasks. RMTs offer a linear time and constant memory alternative to standard Transformers, but their sequential memory update mechanism creates a performance bottleneck. Diagonal Batching unlocks parallelism across segments by reordering computations, allowing for concurrent GPU execution without compromising exact recurrence. The authors demonstrate a speedup of up to 3.3x over a standard LLaMA-1B model and 1.8x over a sequential RMT implementation when processing long sequences (131,072 tokens). The technique is presented as a run-time optimization that doesn't require retraining existing models, making it a practical solution for long-context applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of Diagonal Batching to exploit intra-layer parallelism in *Parallel* RMTs seems reasonably novel in terms of RMT inference optimization.  The core insight of rearranging the computation graph for concurrent execution on a GPU is a valuable contribution. While parallelism techniques are common, the specific application to RMTs to overcome the sequential bottleneck is new.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is moderately significant.  Speeding up inference for long-context models is crucial for real-world applications.  The fact that it requires no retraining is a big win. The results show a substantial improvement in inference speed for models with a segment size of 1024, suggesting that this can be a standard size for deploying to GPU. If this optimization could be used at inference time, then this can be a good approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practicality:** The run-time optimization aspect is a major strength, as it allows existing RMT models to be deployed without additional training costs.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper provides a clear explanation of Diagonal Batching and its advantages. The figures are helpful.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments are comprehensive, demonstrating speedups across different model sizes and sequence lengths. It is nice to see results with open source models.
    *   **Focus:** Focus on a single GPU inference is relevant for deployment with one long context.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Applicability:** It does not apply to RMTs which are more general memory models compared to Parallel RMTs. The authors acknowledge in the limitations section that there are some technical limitations in employing the method to RMTs, which can limit broader adaptation of the method.
    *   **Implementation Details:** Some implementation details are not fully clear, such as, how the GroupedGEMM operations can guarantee the memory update operations in diagonal blocks still preserve layer-level recurrence.
    *   **Scope of the contribution:** This relies on existing work for all of the memory savings, only contributing to execution.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development and deployment of more efficient RMT-based systems, especially in areas where long-context processing is essential. It might encourage further research into scheduling schemes for other recurrent models.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

Diagonal Batching offers a novel and practical approach to accelerating RMT inference. The elimination of the sequential bottleneck, coupled with the ease of deployment (no retraining), makes this a valuable contribution. However, the limited applicability of the technique to only layer-level RMT architectures, as well as some areas for improvement in writing, caps the score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Progressive Tempering Sampler with Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05231v1)
- **Authors**: Severi Rissanen, RuiKang OuYang, Jiajun He, Wenlin Chen, Markus Heinonen, Arno Solin, José Miguel Hernández-Lobato
- **Abstract**: Recent research has focused on designing neural samplers that amortize the process of sampling from unnormalized densities. However, despite significant advancements, they still fall short of the state-of-the-art MCMC approach, Parallel Tempering (PT), when it comes to the efficiency of target evaluations. On the other hand, unlike a well-trained neural sampler, PT yields only dependent samples and needs to be rerun -- at considerable computational cost -- whenever new samples are required. To address these weaknesses, we propose the Progressive Tempering Sampler with Diffusion (PTSD), which trains diffusion models sequentially across temperatures, leveraging the advantages of PT to improve the training of neural samplers. We also introduce a novel method to combine high-temperature diffusion models to generate approximate lower-temperature samples, which are minimally refined using MCMC and used to train the next diffusion model. PTSD enables efficient reuse of sample information across temperature levels while generating well-mixed, uncorrelated samples. Our method significantly improves target evaluation efficiency, outperforming diffusion-based neural samplers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Progressive Tempering Sampler with Diffusion":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Progressive Tempering Sampler with Diffusion (PTSD), a novel approach that integrates Parallel Tempering (PT) with diffusion models for efficient sampling from unnormalized densities. PTSD sequentially trains diffusion models at decreasing temperatures. It leverages a new "temperature guidance" mechanism to enable higher-temperature diffusion models to generate approximate samples for lower temperatures, which are then refined with MCMC and used to train subsequent diffusion models. The method aims to overcome the limitations of both traditional MCMC (slow sampling, dependent samples) and neural samplers (high computational cost for training, often less efficient than PT). The authors demonstrate, through experiments on several target distributions, that PTSD achieves state-of-the-art sample quality while significantly improving target evaluation efficiency compared to other diffusion-based neural samplers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining PT and diffusion models isn't entirely new, as some existing methods fit diffusion models to data generated by PT. However, the *sequential* training of diffusion models across temperatures, the introduction of *temperature guidance* for sample generation, and the integration of importance resampling and local PT refinement steps present a significant advancement. Temperature guidance, in particular, is a well-motivated contribution, allowing the transfer of information across temperature levels in a manner more effective than simple sample swapping in traditional PT. The method represents a clever way of amortizing the computational cost across temperature levels, a problem that is not tackled by existing learned samplers that train only at a specific (target) temperature.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial bottleneck in the field: the high computational cost associated with training neural samplers, especially compared to the efficiency of traditional MCMC methods. By achieving orders-of-magnitude improvements in target evaluation efficiency compared to other diffusion-based neural samplers *while maintaining competitive sample quality*, PTSD demonstrates a practical path towards making neural samplers more viable for real-world applications. The experiments are well-designed and the comparisons are thorough, including analysis of W2 distance, TVD, MMD, and wall clock time.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-motivated approach that addresses a clear weakness in the existing literature.
    *   The temperature guidance mechanism offers a principled way to leverage information from higher temperatures.
    *   The algorithm is well-described and the pseudo-code helps understand the implementation.
    *   The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the efficiency gains over existing methods.
    *   Thorough ablation studies clarify the impact of different components of PTSD.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method still requires training a sequence of diffusion models, which, while more efficient in target evaluations, is still computationally expensive in wall-clock time, especially for very difficult problems. This is acknowledged by the authors.
    *   The performance is sensitive to hyperparameter tuning (e.g., the temperature schedule, the truncation threshold for importance sampling). While PT is also sensitive, this sensitivity combined with diffusion model hyperparameters makes PTSD somewhat cumbersome to use.
    *   The authors admit that on a small set of experiments, BNNEM has a slight advantage on sample quality which they attribute to that model's additional smoothing step, making the gradient surface easier to traverse.
    *   While the paper highlights theoretical connections to existing diffusion model guidance techniques, it lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of convergence properties or guarantees.
*   **Potential Influence:** PTSD has the potential to influence the development of future neural samplers by emphasizing the integration of traditional MCMC techniques and learned models.  It could also lead to more efficient ways of exploring complex energy landscapes in various scientific fields, such as molecular simulations and materials science. A direction forward is to amortize the entire process by also learning the process of choosing temperatures.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

PTSD is a solid contribution that advances the state-of-the-art in neural sampling by effectively bridging the gap between traditional MCMC and learned models. While it has some limitations in wall-clock training time and sensitivity, the significant improvement in target evaluation efficiency and the well-motivated temperature guidance mechanism justify a relatively high score. Its potential to influence the development of more practical and efficient neural samplers is considerable, especially when integrating ideas to amortize the temperature selection process and training.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MesaNet: Sequence Modeling by Locally Optimal Test-Time Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05233v1)
- **Authors**: Johannes von Oswald, Nino Scherrer, Seijin Kobayashi, Luca Versari, Songlin Yang, Maximilian Schlegel, Kaitlin Maile, Yanick Schimpf, Oliver Sieberling, Alexander Meulemans, Rif A. Saurous, Guillaume Lajoie, Charlotte Frenkel, Razvan Pascanu, Blaise Agüera y Arcas, João Sacramento
- **Abstract**: Sequence modeling is currently dominated by causal transformer architectures that use softmax self-attention. Although widely adopted, transformers require scaling memory and compute linearly during inference. A recent stream of work linearized the softmax operation, resulting in powerful recurrent neural network (RNN) models with constant memory and compute costs such as DeltaNet, Mamba or xLSTM. These models can be unified by noting that their recurrent layer dynamics can all be derived from an in-context regression objective, approximately optimized through an online learning rule. Here, we join this line of work and introduce a numerically stable, chunkwise parallelizable version of the recently proposed Mesa layer (von Oswald et al., 2024), and study it in language modeling at the billion-parameter scale. This layer again stems from an in-context loss, but which is now minimized to optimality at every time point using a fast conjugate gradient solver. Through an extensive suite of experiments, we show that optimal test-time training enables reaching lower language modeling perplexity and higher downstream benchmark performance than previous RNNs, especially on tasks requiring long context understanding. This performance gain comes at the cost of additional flops spent during inference time. Our results are therefore intriguingly related to recent trends of increasing test-time compute to improve performance -- here by spending compute to solve sequential optimization problems within the neural network itself.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MesaNet, a sequence modeling architecture that leverages a novel Mesa layer. This layer is a type of recurrent layer inspired by the idea of optimal test-time training. The Mesa layer calculates its output by solving a quadratic optimization problem at each time step, effectively finding the "best" linear map given the context seen so far. The authors design a parallelizable and numerically stable version of the Mesa layer, which allows it to be trained efficiently on large datasets using matrix multiplication accelerators. They show that MesaNet achieves competitive results in language modeling, outperforming existing RNNs with constant memory and compute cost, especially in tasks requiring long context understanding. Furthermore, the layer allocates test-time compute dynamically.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of the Mesa layer, solving an optimization problem at each step, is a continuation of previous work by the same authors [105]. This work builds upon it by addressing key limitations like parallelizability and numerical stability, and shows how it can be implemented efficiently. However, the core underlying idea of solving an in-context regression problem is not entirely new, as it connects to a broader line of research on fast weight programming and test-time adaptation. Concurrent work (Atlas [10]) also corresponds to a sliding-window variant of Mesa.
*   **Significance:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by making a previously proposed Mesa layer practical for large-scale language modeling. Demonstrating that the layer can be trained and deployed at the billion-parameter scale is a significant step. The experimental results showing that MesaNet outperforms other RNNs and approaches the performance of Transformers is promising, particularly for tasks demanding long context understanding. The paper also provides valuable insights into the behavior of modern RNNs, their limitations, and areas where further research is needed. A novel result of this paper is the observation of the benefits of solving an optimization problem at every step in comparison to a gradual optimization with gradient descent.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Implementation:** The parallelizable and numerically stable version of the Mesa layer is a crucial engineering achievement.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive set of experiments on synthetic and natural language tasks, including language modeling and downstream benchmarks.
    *   **In-depth Analysis:** The authors go beyond simple performance comparisons and investigate the behavior of different architectures, revealing insights about their strengths and weaknesses. The dynamic allocation of test-time compute is an interesting and potentially valuable feature.
    *   Dynamic allocation of test-time compute enables a novel trade-off.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The core idea of the Mesa layer is not entirely new.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The Mesa layer introduces a significant computational overhead during inference, potentially limiting its applicability in resource-constrained environments. The gain in performance may not justify the increased computational cost in all scenarios.
    *   **Transformer Gap:** While MesaNet outperforms other RNNs, it does not fully close the performance gap with Transformers, particularly on tasks that require strong in-context recall.
    *   **Performance in tasks with global reasoning:** Although it exhibits promising early perplexity and strong language modeling, the mesa layer underperforms transformers and SWA on benchmarks which require global reasoning in the long context.
    *   **Implementation specifics:** A large set of non-generic parameters (lower-bound and upper-bound on lambda)

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper will influence research by:
    *   Inspiring further exploration of optimal test-time training techniques for sequence modeling.
    *   Providing a practical implementation of the Mesa layer that can be used as a foundation for future research.
    *   Highlighting the importance of considering dynamic compute allocation in neural network design.
    *   Focusing RNN research in global language tasks to surpass transformer capabilities.
    *   Influencing the scaling laws of future models.

*   **Justification for Score:**
The paper's significance lies in its practical implementation and empirical evaluation of a technique that has potential benefits, but is not currently transformative due to the cost during inference. The extensive experiments offer valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of recurrent sequence models. A key novel result is the ability to trade-off compute at test time and overall performance. However, the limited novelty and a reliance on an architecture that has limited performance in tasks which need global context information is a crucial limitation.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Aligning Latent Spaces with Flow Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05240v1)
- **Authors**: Yizhuo Li, Yuying Ge, Yixiao Ge, Ying Shan, Ping Luo
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a novel framework for aligning learnable latent spaces to arbitrary target distributions by leveraging flow-based generative models as priors. Our method first pretrains a flow model on the target features to capture the underlying distribution. This fixed flow model subsequently regularizes the latent space via an alignment loss, which reformulates the flow matching objective to treat the latents as optimization targets. We formally prove that minimizing this alignment loss establishes a computationally tractable surrogate objective for maximizing a variational lower bound on the log-likelihood of latents under the target distribution. Notably, the proposed method eliminates computationally expensive likelihood evaluations and avoids ODE solving during optimization. As a proof of concept, we demonstrate in a controlled setting that the alignment loss landscape closely approximates the negative log-likelihood of the target distribution. We further validate the effectiveness of our approach through large-scale image generation experiments on ImageNet with diverse target distributions, accompanied by detailed discussions and ablation studies. With both theoretical and empirical validation, our framework paves a new way for latent space alignment.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces a novel framework for aligning learnable latent spaces with arbitrary target distributions by leveraging pre-trained flow-based generative models as priors. The method adapts the flow matching objective to treat latent variables as optimization targets, establishing a computationally tractable alignment loss. It theoretically demonstrates that minimizing this loss serves as a proxy for maximizing a variational lower bound on the log-likelihood of the latents under the target distribution. The approach avoids expensive likelihood evaluations and ODE solving during optimization, offering an efficient mechanism for guiding latent spaces towards desired structures. The paper validates its effectiveness through toy experiments and large-scale image generation on ImageNet using diverse target distributions, accompanied by discussions and ablation studies.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a conceptually sound and practically valuable approach to latent space alignment. The core novelty lies in reformulating the flow matching objective to directly optimize latents, thereby offering a computationally efficient alternative to direct likelihood maximization or adversarial training. The theoretical justification connecting the alignment loss to a variational lower bound provides a strong foundation for the method.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The latent space alignment via flow matching objective reformulation is a novel contribution.
*   **Theoretical Foundation:** Connecting the alignment loss to a variational lower bound provides a strong justification.
*   **Computational Efficiency:** Avoiding expensive likelihood evaluations and ODE solving is a significant advantage.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The method is validated through diverse experiments, including large-scale ImageNet generation.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the method and theoretical results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependence on Pre-trained Flow Model:** The framework relies on a well-trained flow model, which can be computationally expensive to obtain. The performance is thus contingent on the quality of the flow model.
*   **Dimension Mismatch Heuristic:** The linear projection used to address dimension mismatch, while supported by the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma, may not always be optimal and could introduce information loss. Alternative approaches such as more sophisticated dimensionality reduction techniques might be explored.
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis of C(y):** Although the paper provides a theoretical analysis, there are a few limitations to this. First, there is no guarantee that maximizing C(y) is sufficient, or even necessary, for improving the variational lower bound. This limits our confidence in the theoretical framework. Also, Lemma 1 and Theorem 1 provide little insight on why L_align works in practice, and rely on very specific and unrealistic assumptions.
*   **Limited Scope of Prior Distributions:** The experiments mainly focus on using pre-trained feature extractors (visual and textual) as target distributions. Exploring other types of prior distributions, such as those based on structured knowledge graphs, could further enhance the applicability of the method.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a crucial problem in representation learning: ensuring that latent spaces possess desired structural properties. The proposed method offers a practical and theoretically grounded approach to incorporating rich distributional priors into latent models. The potential applications of this framework span various domains requiring structured representation learning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field. The novelty in the formulation of the alignment loss is significant, and the theoretical justification bolsters its validity. While the method has weaknesses, especially regarding the dependence on pre-trained models and the dimension mismatch heuristic, the experiments demonstrate its effectiveness across various settings. The paper provides a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners working on representation learning and generative modeling.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SECNEURON: Reliable and Flexible Abuse Control in Local LLMs via Hybrid Neuron Encryption
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05242v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiqiang Wang, Haohua Du, Junyang Wang, Haifeng Sun, Kaiwen Guo, Haikuo Yu, Chao Liu, Xiang-Yang Li
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) with diverse capabilities are increasingly being deployed in local environments, presenting significant security and controllability challenges. These locally deployed LLMs operate outside the direct control of developers, rendering them more susceptible to abuse. Existing mitigation techniques mainly designed for cloud-based LLM services are frequently circumvented or ineffective in deployer-controlled environments. We propose SECNEURON, the first framework that seamlessly embeds classic access control within the intrinsic capabilities of LLMs, achieving reliable, cost-effective, flexible, and certified abuse control for local deployed LLMs. SECNEURON employs neuron-level encryption and selective decryption to dynamically control the task-specific capabilities of LLMs, limiting unauthorized task abuse without compromising others. We first design a task-specific neuron extraction mechanism to decouple logically related neurons and construct a layered policy tree for handling coupled neurons. We then introduce a flexible and efficient hybrid encryption framework for millions of neurons in LLMs. Finally, we developed a distribution-based decrypted neuron detection mechanism on ciphertext to ensure the effectiveness of partially decrypted LLMs. We proved that SECNEURON satisfies IND-CPA Security and Collusion Resistance Security under the Task Controllability Principle. Experiments on various task settings show that SECNEURON limits unauthorized task accuracy to below 25% while keeping authorized accuracy loss with 2%. Using an unauthorized Code task example, the accuracy of abuse-related malicious code generation was reduced from 59% to 15%. SECNEURON also mitigates unauthorized data leakage, reducing PII extraction rates to below 5% and membership inference to random guesses.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SECNEURON: Reliable and Flexible Abuse Control in Local LLMs via Hybrid Neuron Encryption":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SECNEURON, a novel framework designed to control and mitigate abuse in locally deployed Large Language Models (LLMs). SECNEURON leverages neuron-level encryption and selective decryption to dynamically control the task-specific capabilities of LLMs. This approach aims to limit unauthorized task usage without compromising authorized functionalities. The framework consists of three key components: a task-specific neuron extraction mechanism for decoupling logically related neurons, a hybrid encryption framework for efficient encryption of millions of neurons, and a distribution-based decrypted neuron detection mechanism to ensure the effectiveness of partially decrypted LLMs. The paper claims that SECNEURON offers flexibility, reliability, and certified abuse control, enables one-time encryption and transmission, and supports multi-party selective decryption with minimal overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of directly embedding access control within the LLM's internal workings, specifically at the neuron level, is a significant departure from existing methods that primarily focus on input/output filtering, safety alignment, or model fine-tuning/unlearning. The hybrid encryption approach, combining attribute-based encryption (CP-ABE) for key management with symmetric encryption (AES-CTR) for efficient neuron encryption, demonstrates a good understanding of the trade-offs between flexibility and performance. The decrypted neuron detection is also a clever way to deal with ciphertext leakage.
*   **Significance:** The problem of abuse control in locally deployed LLMs is becoming increasingly important as these models are widely deployed in environments outside the control of developers. SECNEURON addresses this challenge by offering a potential solution that provides fine-grained control over model capabilities, allowing for customized deployment scenarios and mitigating the risks of unauthorized task abuse and data extraction.
*   **Technical Depth:** The paper demonstrates strong technical depth by presenting a detailed framework with clearly defined components and algorithms. The authors provide a theoretical analysis of the security properties of SECNEURON, proving IND-CPA security and collusion resistance under certain conditions.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The paper includes extensive experimental results across various tasks and models, demonstrating the effectiveness of SECNEURON in limiting unauthorized task accuracy, preventing data leakage, and mitigating malicious code generation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity and Scalability:** While the hybrid encryption approach is innovative, the reliance on CP-ABE for key management could introduce complexity and scalability challenges, especially for LLMs with millions of neurons and complex permission structures. The paper claims millisecond-level key generation, but the overhead of CP-ABE management could be a bottleneck in practice.
*   **Practical Deployment Considerations:** The paper focuses primarily on the technical aspects of the framework and provides limited discussion of the practical considerations for deployment, such as the integration with existing LLM infrastructure, the cost of implementing the neuron extraction mechanism, and the impact on model inference speed.
*   **Overly Strong Claims:** While the experimental results are promising, some of the claims made in the abstract and introduction (e.g., "certified abuse control") may be overly strong. The security guarantees provided by SECNEURON depend on several assumptions, such as the validity of the Task Controllability Principle and the security of the underlying cryptographic primitives.
*   **Evaluation Metrics:** The evaluation uses Accuracy as a primary measure which might not always correlate well with the real-world abuse scenarios, for instance, for malicious code generation tasks. More realistic task-specific metrics could provide a more accurate picture.
*   **Limited Comparisons to Baselines:** The paper argues against suitability of prior methods for the scenario but doesn't implement or explicitly compare with those for metrics relevant in the local LLM context.

**Overall Assessment:**

SECNEURON presents a valuable contribution to the field by proposing a novel approach to abuse control in locally deployed LLMs. The framework offers a promising combination of flexibility, reliability, and certified security, addressing a crucial challenge in the increasingly decentralized landscape of LLM deployment. However, the paper could be improved by addressing the scalability and deployment challenges, refining the claims made about the security properties of the framework, and conducting a more comprehensive evaluation of its practical performance.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The score of 7.5 reflects the significant novelty and potential impact of SECNEURON, while acknowledging the limitations and areas for improvement discussed above. The paper introduces a genuinely innovative approach to a relevant problem, provides a solid technical foundation, and presents promising experimental results. However, the challenges related to scalability, practical deployment, overly strong claims, and baselines need to be addressed to fully realize the potential of SECNEURON and establish its significance within the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### On the Convergence of Gradient Descent on Learning Transformers with Residual Connections
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05249v1)
- **Authors**: Zhen Qin, Jinxin Zhou, Zhihui Zhu
- **Abstract**: Transformer models have emerged as fundamental tools across various scientific and engineering disciplines, owing to their outstanding performance in diverse applications. Despite this empirical success, the theoretical foundations of Transformers remain relatively underdeveloped, particularly in understanding their training dynamics. Existing research predominantly examines isolated components--such as self-attention mechanisms and feedforward networks--without thoroughly investigating the interdependencies between these components, especially when residual connections are present. In this paper, we aim to bridge this gap by analyzing the convergence behavior of a structurally complete yet single-layer Transformer, comprising self-attention, a feedforward network, and residual connections. We demonstrate that, under appropriate initialization, gradient descent exhibits a linear convergence rate, where the convergence speed is determined by the minimum and maximum singular values of the output matrix from the attention layer. Moreover, our analysis reveals that residual connections serve to ameliorate the ill-conditioning of this output matrix, an issue stemming from the low-rank structure imposed by the softmax operation, thereby promoting enhanced optimization stability. We also extend our theoretical findings to a multi-layer Transformer architecture, confirming the linear convergence rate of gradient descent under suitable initialization. Empirical results corroborate our theoretical insights, illustrating the beneficial role of residual connections in promoting convergence stability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a theoretical analysis of the convergence of gradient descent when training single-layer and multi-layer Transformer models with self-attention, feedforward networks, and crucially, residual connections. It demonstrates that with appropriate initialization, gradient descent exhibits linear convergence. The convergence rate is linked to the minimum and maximum singular values of the attention layer's output. A key finding is that residual connections help to mitigate ill-conditioning arising from the softmax operation's low-rank structure, thereby improving optimization stability. The theoretical findings are supported by empirical results on synthetic and real-world data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Focus on an important area:** Understanding the training dynamics of Transformers is crucial for developing more efficient and reliable training methods. The paper addresses a significant gap by analyzing the combined effects of self-attention, feedforward networks, and residual connections, rather than examining these components in isolation.
    *   **Rigorous theoretical analysis:** The paper provides a detailed theoretical analysis backed by mathematical proofs. It clearly states assumptions and presents a formal convergence guarantee.
    *   **Insightful findings regarding residual connections:** The explanation of how residual connections combat ill-conditioning caused by rank collapse is insightful and provides a strong theoretical justification for their empirical effectiveness.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The paper includes empirical experiments to support its theoretical claims, which strengthens the findings. Using both synthetic and real-world data adds to the robustness of the validation.
    *   **Extension to multi-layer Transformers:** The extension of the theory to multi-layer architectures, even if informal, adds significant value to the contribution as the findings become more applicable to practical scenarios.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplifying assumptions:** The model studied is simplified to a single layer and a single-head Transformer, limiting the immediate applicability to the complex architectures used in practice. While the authors extend the theory to multi-layer transformers, it's an informal result and lacks the rigor of the single-layer analysis. The omission of layer normalization, a crucial component in modern transformers, is another limitation.
    *   **Strong initialization conditions:** The convergence guarantee relies on specific initialization conditions, which might be difficult to satisfy in all practical scenarios. The analysis assumes either full row rank or full column rank initial matrices which can be difficult. A discussion of how sensitive the theoretical guarantees are to deviations from the assumed initialization would be helpful.
    *   **Limited real-world data experiments:** The experiments on real-world data (Jena Climate Dataset) are relatively simple and could be expanded. A more comprehensive set of experiments on diverse datasets and tasks would further strengthen the validation. Also, the analysis focuses on the convergence, with some discussion regarding the global minima. An expansion on this could also further aid in understanding.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a valuable contribution by offering a theoretical understanding of how residual connections affect the convergence of gradient descent in Transformers, especially considering the interplay of self-attention and feedforward networks. The novelty lies in the integrated analysis rather than isolating components.
    *   The theoretical explanation of how residual connections alleviate ill-conditioning resulting from rank collapse in self-attention mechanisms is particularly significant. This contributes to a deeper understanding of the role of residual connections beyond simply easing gradient flow.
    *   While the simplifying assumptions limit the direct applicability to large-scale models, the paper lays a theoretical foundation that can potentially be extended to more complex scenarios. The empirical verification is a good starting point, however, there is still room for exploration in order to fully validate this claim.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the theoretical understanding of Transformers, but it is limited by simplifying assumptions and the scope of the empirical validation. The key strengths are in the rigorous analysis and the insightful explanation of the role of residual connections. However, the strong initialization requirements and the limitations in the model architecture hold the paper back from being truly groundbreaking.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LeanPO: Lean Preference Optimization for Likelihood Alignment in Video-LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05260v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaodong Wang, Jinfa Huang, Li Yuan, Peixi Peng
- **Abstract**: Most Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs) adopt preference alignment techniques, e.g., DPO~\citep{rafailov2024dpo}, to optimize the reward margin between a winning response ($y_w$) and a losing response ($y_l$). However, the likelihood displacement observed in DPO indicates that both $\log \pi_\theta (y_w\mid x)$ and $\log \pi_\theta (y_l\mid x) $ often decrease during training, inadvertently boosting the probabilities of non-target responses. In this paper, we systematically revisit this phenomenon from LLMs to Video-LLMs, showing that it intensifies when dealing with the redundant complexity of video content. To alleviate the impact of this phenomenon, we propose \emph{Lean Preference Optimization} (LeanPO), a reference-free approach that reformulates the implicit reward as the average likelihood of the response with respect to the policy model. A key component of LeanPO is the reward-trustworthiness correlated self-generated preference data pipeline, which carefully infuses relevant prior knowledge into the model while continuously refining the preference data via self-reflection. This allows the policy model to obtain high-quality paired data and accurately estimate the newly defined reward, thus mitigating the unintended drop. In addition, we introduce a dynamic label smoothing strategy that mitigates the impact of noise in responses from diverse video content, preventing the model from overfitting to spurious details. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LeanPO significantly enhances the performance of state-of-the-art Video-LLMs, consistently boosting baselines of varying capacities with minimal additional training overhead. Moreover, LeanPO offers a simple yet effective solution for aligning Video-LLM preferences with human trustworthiness, paving the way toward the reliable and efficient Video-LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "LEANPO: Lean Preference Optimization for Likelihood Alignment in Video-LLMs" addresses the issue of likelihood displacement in preference alignment techniques (like DPO) when applied to Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs). The authors observe that the likelihood of both winning and losing responses decreases during DPO training, exacerbating the problem in the context of complex video content. To tackle this, they propose Lean Preference Optimization (LEANPO), a reference-free approach that reformulates the implicit reward as the average likelihood of the response with respect to the policy model. A key component is a reward-trustworthiness correlated self-generated preference data pipeline that injects prior knowledge and refines preference data via self-reflection. Dynamic label smoothing is used to mitigate noise from diverse video content. Experimental results demonstrate that LEANPO enhances the performance of state-of-the-art Video-LLMs with minimal additional training overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its adaptation and reformulation of preference optimization for the specific challenges presented by Video-LLMs. While likelihood displacement has been studied in LLMs, the paper makes a clear and substantiated case that the problem is amplified in the video domain. The LEANPO approach, with its reference-free reward formulation and reward-trustworthiness correlated self-generated preference data pipeline, presents a practical approach to address this challenge. The dynamic label smoothing strategy adds another layer of refinement.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it directly addresses a practical challenge hindering the development of reliable and efficient Video-LLMs: the misalignment between training objectives and inference metrics due to likelihood displacement. By providing a simple yet effective solution (LEANPO), the paper facilitates alignment with human trustworthiness and paves the way for improved video understanding and reasoning in these models. The experimental results, showing consistent improvements across various video benchmarks, further reinforce the practical relevance of the proposed method.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the issue of likelihood displacement in Video-LLMs.
    *   Well-motivated solution: LEANPO is grounded in a solid understanding of the limitations of existing methods.
    *   Comprehensive experiments: Experiments cover multiple benchmarks and ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   Simplicity: LEANPO is reference-free, making it simpler to implement and deploy compared to reward-model-based methods.
    *   Generalizability: Generalization analysis on Image-LLMs and Qwen-VL shows the method is applicable to different model families.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental Improvement: While the improvements demonstrated are significant, the gains, especially when already based on TPO, are smaller and may be perceived as somewhat incremental, especially on the LLaVa-Video experiments
    *   Limited theoretical analysis: The paper could benefit from a more detailed theoretical analysis of why LEANPO is effective in mitigating likelihood displacement. Although empirical evidence supports the claims, a more formal explanation would further strengthen the paper.
    *   Data generation process details: Certain aspects of the reward-trustworthiness correlated data pipeline (e.g. video augmentation techniques applied, prompts) could benefit from a clearer description to enhance reproducibility.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    The paper can have a considerable influence because its findings will likely improve the performance of a wide range of Video-LLM tasks. The approach is conceptually simple and practically effective. This facilitates adaptation and incorporation by the broader research community.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**
The paper presents a well-motivated solution to a significant problem in Video-LLM alignment. The empirical results support the effectiveness of LEANPO. The main weakness is the limited theoretical depth explaining why the proposed method works, and some details may be missing to facilitate easy reproduction. Considering the practical improvements demonstrated and potential for future adaptations, the score of 7.5 reflects a solid contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Teaming in the AI Era: AI-Augmented Frameworks for Forming, Simulating, and Optimizing Human Teams
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05265v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammed Almutairi
- **Abstract**: Effective teamwork is essential across diverse domains. During the team formation stage, a key challenge is forming teams that effectively balance user preferences with task objectives to enhance overall team satisfaction. In the team performing stage, maintaining cohesion and engagement is critical for sustaining high team performance. However, existing computational tools and algorithms for team optimization often rely on static data inputs, narrow algorithmic objectives, or solutions tailored for specific contexts, failing to account for the dynamic interplay of team members personalities, evolving goals, and changing individual preferences. Therefore, teams may encounter member dissatisfaction, as purely algorithmic assignments can reduce members commitment to team goals or experience suboptimal engagement due to the absence of timely, personalized guidance to help members adjust their behaviors and interactions as team dynamics evolve. Ultimately, these challenges can lead to reduced overall team performance. My Ph.D. dissertation aims to develop AI-augmented team optimization frameworks and practical systems that enhance team satisfaction, engagement, and performance. First, I propose a team formation framework that leverages a multi-armed bandit algorithm to iteratively refine team composition based on user preferences, ensuring alignment between individual needs and collective team goals to enhance team satisfaction. Second, I introduce tAIfa (Team AI Feedback Assistant), an AI-powered system that utilizes large language models (LLMs) to deliver immediate, personalized feedback to both teams and individual members, enhancing cohesion and engagement. Finally, I present PuppeteerLLM, an LLM-based simulation framework that simulates multi-agent teams to model complex team dynamics within realistic environments, incorporating task-driven collaboration and long-term coordination.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided research proposal:

**Summary:**

The proposal outlines a PhD dissertation focusing on AI-augmented frameworks for forming, simulating, and optimizing human teams.  The research addresses three key stages of teamwork: formation, performance, and understanding team dynamics.  The proposal aims to develop: (1) a team formation framework using a multi-armed bandit approach to incorporate user preferences; (2) tAlfa, an LLM-powered system providing real-time, personalized feedback to teams and individuals to improve engagement and cohesion; and (3) PuppeteerLLM, an LLM-based simulation framework for modeling complex team dynamics. The research uses a human-centered approach to improve team satisfaction, engagement, and performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The combination of the three proposed components (team formation via MAB, real-time feedback via LLM, and simulation via LLM) has potential novelty.  While each component has existing literature, the integration and specific application within team dynamics seems to offer something unique. The use of multi-armed bandits for team formation is relatively less explored compared to basic recommender systems.  LLM-based feedback and agent simulation are burgeoning areas, but the specific design and integration of tAlfa and PuppeteerLLM could offer novel contributions.

**Significance:** The proposal addresses a significant challenge: enhancing teamwork in a world increasingly reliant on virtual and hybrid collaboration.  Improving team satisfaction, engagement, and performance are essential for various domains, including education, business, and healthcare.  If successful, the proposed frameworks could provide practical tools and insights for team leaders and organizations. The potential impact on improving team effectiveness is significant. A strong emphasis is placed on human-centered design, addressing the limitations of existing algorithmic approaches that sometimes neglect human preferences and engagement. The proposal demonstrates an understanding of related work and a clear direction for future work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs:** The proposal heavily relies on LLMs, which are known for potential biases, hallucinations, and a lack of true understanding. The proposal does not explicitly address how it will mitigate these limitations.
*   **Evaluation Rigor:** While preliminary results for tAlfa are presented, the proposal lacks detailed information about evaluation metrics and baselines for all three components.  A stronger emphasis on rigorous empirical validation is needed. The proposal outlines an offline evaluation of the UCB team formation algorithm but needs a more concrete plan for evaluating the interactive UCB-based system.
*   **Generalizability:** The experiments are to be conducted using Discord. Will the findings generalize to other collaboration platforms?
*   **Limited Specificity:** While the objectives are clearly stated, the specific implementation details of the LLM prompts, feedback mechanisms, and simulation environment could be more elaborated. More details on the specific UCB algorithm configuration and tuning are also needed.
*   **Lack of Focus on Ethical Concerns:** The proposal omits discussion of the ethical implications of AI-driven team optimization, such as the potential for bias, surveillance, or manipulation of team members.

**Justification for Score:**

The proposal shows promise due to its comprehensive approach to addressing team dynamics with a combination of techniques. The integration of LLMs is timely and potentially impactful. However, the weaknesses related to LLM biases, evaluation rigor, and limited detail prevent it from scoring higher.

### Micro-Act: Mitigate Knowledge Conflict in Question Answering via Actionable Self-Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05278v1)
- **Authors**: Nan Huo, Jinyang Li, Bowen Qin, Ge Qu, Xiaolong Li, Xiaodong Li, Chenhao Ma, Reynold Cheng
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems commonly suffer from Knowledge Conflicts, where retrieved external knowledge contradicts the inherent, parametric knowledge of large language models (LLMs). It adversely affects performance on downstream tasks such as question answering (QA). Existing approaches often attempt to mitigate conflicts by directly comparing two knowledge sources in a side-by-side manner, but this can overwhelm LLMs with extraneous or lengthy contexts, ultimately hindering their ability to identify and mitigate inconsistencies. To address this issue, we propose Micro-Act a framework with a hierarchical action space that automatically perceives context complexity and adaptively decomposes each knowledge source into a sequence of fine-grained comparisons. These comparisons are represented as actionable steps, enabling reasoning beyond the superficial context. Through extensive experiments on five benchmark datasets, Micro-Act consistently achieves significant increase in QA accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines across all 5 datasets and 3 conflict types, especially in temporal and semantic types where all baselines fail significantly. More importantly, Micro-Act exhibits robust performance on non-conflict questions simultaneously, highlighting its practical value in real-world RAG applications.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Micro-Act: Mitigate Knowledge Conflict in Question Answering via Actionable Self-Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of knowledge conflicts in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems for question answering (QA). Knowledge conflicts occur when retrieved external knowledge contradicts the inherent, parametric knowledge of Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors propose a novel framework called MICRO-ACT, which uses a hierarchical action space to automatically perceive context complexity and decompose each knowledge source into a sequence of fine-grained comparisons. These comparisons are represented as actionable steps that enable reasoning beyond the superficial context. The approach dynamically adjusts granularity through decomposition actions at both the model and action level, enabling precise conflict detection across different granularity levels. The paper demonstrates through extensive experiments on five benchmark datasets that MICRO-ACT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in QA accuracy, especially in temporal and semantic conflict types. Crucially, it also shows robustness in non-conflict scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The key innovation of the paper lies in its hierarchical action space and the adaptive granularity adjustment through decomposition actions. Existing approaches primarily rely on side-by-side comparisons or generation-aided reasoning, which can be overwhelmed by extraneous context or limited by manually crafted instructions. MICRO-ACT’s ability to dynamically perceive context complexity and decompose knowledge sources into actionable steps is a novel approach for mitigating knowledge conflicts.

**Significance:** The problem of knowledge conflicts is a critical challenge in RAG systems, affecting the reliability and factual accuracy of LLM responses. By addressing this challenge, MICRO-ACT has the potential to significantly improve the performance of RAG systems in downstream tasks such as QA. The experimental results demonstrate that MICRO-ACT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across various datasets and conflict types. More importantly, the observed robustness in non-conflict scenarios highlights the practical value of MICRO-ACT in real-world RAG applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The hierarchical action space and adaptive granularity adjustment are innovative techniques for mitigating knowledge conflicts.
*   **Strong Performance:** The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in QA accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines across various datasets and conflict types.
*   **Robustness:** The framework exhibits robust performance in non-conflict scenarios, highlighting its practical value in real-world applications.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the limitations of existing approaches and the benefits of MICRO-ACT.
*   **Understanding Complexity:** The work demonstrates the ability of Micro-Act to effectively detect complexity and dynamically adjust granularity

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** While the authors justify the additional computational cost of MICRO-ACT, the increased token usage and inference time may be a concern for resource-constrained applications. The marginal cost will be unacceptable for certain contexts.

*   **Error Types:** Some error types, primarily contextual distraction, do still exist and limits effectiveness. While this isn't a fundamental flaw, it doesn't demonstrate full mitigation.

*   **English Language Focus:** The evaluation focuses primarily on English language contexts, and the effectiveness of decomposition strategies might vary across different languages and cultural contexts.

*   **Limited Generality:** The paper could be made more general and not only apply to the QA case.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in the following areas:

*   Development of more robust and reliable RAG systems.
*   Design of adaptive strategies for mitigating knowledge conflicts in LLMs.
*   Exploration of hierarchical action spaces and decomposition techniques for natural language processing tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to addressing a critical challenge in RAG systems. The strong experimental results and the demonstrated robustness of the framework justify a high score. However, some limitations remain with respect to its computational cost and scope of evaluation. Taking all of these factors into account:
